Marvel fans want James Gunn back after his DC departure: "Start drafting the script for Guardians of the Galaxy 4"

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Marvel fans want James Gunn back in the MCU

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After four years, James Gunn is stepping down as DC Studios co-CEO alongside Peter Safran, but Marvel fans are taking this as a win, as many want the Guardians of the Galaxy director to come back to the MCU.

"Kevin Feige, it's time to bring James Gunn back into the fold," said one fan on Reddit just hours after the news broke. Others seem to agree, as another added, "The people acting like Gunn is awful and don’t want him returning to Marvel are fucking crazy. All three Guardians of the Galaxy movies are great, and he was a great second or third unit director to have on hand for the bigger projects. You’d be crazy to not welcome him back. He has still done cosmic Marvel the best when it comes to the movies."

One fan on Twitter let Gunn know exactly what he should do next, writing, "start drafting the script for Guardians of the Galaxy 4." Another said, "James Gunn no longer DC head means Guardians of the Galaxy 4 confirmed at Marvel?" Let's go."

Gunn started working with Marvel in 2012 when he was hired to co-write and direct the first Guardians of the Galaxy, which was released in 2014. The filmmaker went on to direct scenes in Doctor Strange and wrote and directed the second Guardians movie. However, just as he was slated to direct the third movie, Disney fired Gunn from Marvel Studios in 2018 due to resurfaced social media posts.

Gunn was rehired by Marvel in 2019 and later directed Guardians of the Galaxy Vol.3. But he slowly made the jump to DC, writing and directing the 2021 movie The Suicide Squad and then the HBO show Peacemaker. Then in 2022, Gunn was made co-CEO of DC Studios alongside his long-term collaborator Safran. However, with Gunn now stepping down as DC boss, perhaps the filmmaker will jump ship again.

The DC shake-up comes after Skydance acquired Warner Bros. earlier this year. Gunn and Safran will be replaced by former Marvel Studios executive Jeremy Latcham on December 1, 2026. In a statement posted on Instagram, Gunn said he feels relieved and that he "disliked the at-times corporate nature of the job" and had "difficulty" with "dividing my time between projects" and his role as CEO.

Gunn only launched the DCU: Chapter One with Superman last year. Though reports suggest that the DCU will be dismantled, it looks like projects that are currently in the works will still go forward, including Man of Tomorrow, The People v. Gorilla Grodd, and Creature Commandos season 2.

For more, check out our guide on how to watch the DC movies in order, and keep up with upcoming DC movies and shows announced before Gunn's departure.

QPR’s Top 3 players of the 2026/27 season so far, including resurgent 6-goal/assist star

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

QPR sit 8th in the Championship table with the latest international break upon us. The Hoops have had some patchy form in recent weeks, but they enter the break off the back of a 2-1 win over Lincoln City.

Julien Stephan and co will be pleased with their efforts to date, but there’s room for improvement. The time without league football offers the R’s a chance to put some work in on the training ground, while we can assess the season so far and pick out some standout stars.

In no particular order, we put forward QPR’s top three players of the 2026/27 season so far…

Ronnie Edwards’ fine loan at QPR in the 2024/25 season meant they knew what to expect when he returned permanently earlier this year. He reclaimed a key role swiftly, and the 23-year-old has gone from strength to strength this term.

Used in defensive midfield and centre-back by Stephan, Edwards is one of the Hoops’ most influential players. As per FotMob‘s data, he stands out in possession as he ranks among the Championship’s best centre-backs for successful passes (767), long ball completion rate (62.1%), interceptions (20) and recoveries (65).

His best displays include the big home wins over Swansea City and Derby County, and the 0-0 draw at Charlton Athletic. Edwards has been consistent all season though, proving his star quality at this level.

Throughout his second stint at QPR, Paul Smyth has endured some tough spells. After three years, some may have expected the Northern Irishman to be phased out of the first XI by the likes of Harvey Vale, Karamoko Dembele, Kwame Poku or Koki Saito, but that has not been the case.

Smyth has retained a regular role in Stephan’s side, and it is not hard to see why. The resurgent 29-year-old shows the commitment and endeavour Stephan desires from his attackers, and he’s added more end product to his game with three goals and three assists in his 14 league appearances this season.

He is tied with Richard Kone at the top of the goal contribution charts at Loftus Road and ranks in the top three of Hoops players for big chances created (3), xG on target (2.0) and expected assists (1.6). Smyth has also won three penalties – more than any other player in the Championship (FotMob).

Last but not least is Pierce Charles, whose influence from goalkeeper cannot be overrstated. The Northern Irish prospect arrived as a highly-regarded talent on loan from Manchester City after his Sheffield Wednesday breakthrough, and he has not disappointed.

Charles has work to do with his shot-stopping, as shown by an xG prevented of -2.47 and a save percentage of 67.9%. However, he makes this list because of how his abilities with the ball have changed the way QPR play.

His distribution ability makes Stephan’s side so much more comfortable at the back and they have more routes to the attack thanks to his range of passing. No Championship goalkeeper has made more than his 564 successful passes (FotMob) and he has fantastic numbers in his pass completion rate (77.7%) and long pass accuracy (46.6%).

It has been a real team effort from QPR to start the season in strong fashion. There were some impressive performers overlooked for inclusion here.

Captain Jimmy Dunne has been key with his physical presence and leadership from the back. Midfielder Glen Kamara has made a positive impact since his arrival as well, and if he continues in his recent form, he will be firmly in the player of the season conversation at Loftus Road.

Ilias Chair and Boy Kemper deserve early mentions too, but injuries have limited their involvement compared to others. Kealey Adamson has been one of the most improved players when stepping in at full-back for the R’s.

Liverpool star’s agent confirms talks are underway over prospective new contract

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The agent of one Liverpool player has confirmed that ‘preliminary talks’ are underway over a prospective new contract at Anfield for his client.

There has been much speculation in recent months regarding the long-term future of Alexis Mac Allister, who’s voiced his dismay at not being offered a new deal by the club earlier this year.

However, another South American stalwart in Alisson Becker revealed over the past couple of days that he’d be happy to remain on Merseyside beyond the expiry of his current terms next June.

The goalkeeper’s agent Ze Maria has indicated that negotiations over a contract renewal for the 34-year-old have commenced (albeit at an early stage) and that the Brazil international is very keen to remain with Liverpool.

Alisson’s representative told Egyptian news outlet WinWin: “Alisson loves Liverpool very much. We are in preliminary talks to renew the contract.”

(Photo by Justin Setterfield/Getty Images)

Following on from the Reds’ number 1 expressing his desire to extend his contract, this claim from his agent represents another positive sign that the veteran stopper will continue with LFC beyond next summer.

The Brazilian has been world-class throughout his eight-and-a-bit years at Anfield so far and, despite a couple of costly errors in recent games, was back to his exceptional best in the weekend victory over Crystal Palace as he pulled off a string of fantastic saves.

Liverpool tend not to hand out lucrative contract renewals to players in their mid-30s, but just as they made an exception for Mo Salah in April 2025, we wouldn’t rule out them doing the same for Alisson if he continues to exept.

The goalkeeper is currently on £250,000 per week, with only six of his teammates earning more at present (Capology), so it’d likely require another significant financial commitment to keep him at the club for another season or two.

It’ll likely take another few months before we know for certain whether or not he’ll still be a Reds player next term, but for now the indications are positive that we won’t have to say farewell to the legendary ‘keeper in six months’ time and can instead look forward to even more brilliance from him at Anfield after that.

EFL club eyeing ambitious stadium move

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Stevenage knocked on the door of League One promotion last season, reaching the play-off semi-finals, and now the club are wanting to continue on their upward trajectory off the pitch, as well as on it with an ambitious stadium move.

Stevenage Borough, as they were then known, established itself as a football club in 1976 following the bankruptcy of Stevenage Athletic and the club, now in its 50th year, appears to continue to be going from strength to strength.

The phoenix club has risen from tenth-tier in the English football pyramid all the way to the third-tier, first entering into the Football League in the 2010-11 campaign – and they have not been out of it since.

In the 2011-12 season, following back-to-back promotions, Stevenage finished sixth in League One, which had been their best ever finish until equalling that last season ahead of a defeat to Stockport County in the play-off semi-finals.

With the club continuing to overperform on the pitch through impressive decision-making and general running of the club, ‘Boro are now seeking to take steps off the pitch to sustain themselves at their current level and beyond.

When Stevenage Borough, now just Stevenage, was formed 50 years ago, the club was forced to play at the town’s King George V playing fields due to a dispute before returning to previous home, Broadhall Way, in 1980.

Broadhall Way, which has gone on to be called the Lamex Stadium and now the SPORTA Arena, has remained their home ever since but, in a club statement released on Tuesday, chairman Phil Wallace has outlined ambitions to move on with the hope of increased income to sustain their competitiveness.

Wallace says: “For all the progress we have made, however, there is one fundamental part of the club that has not kept pace with our growth; our stadium.

"We are still operating on the same footprint that was provided 50 years ago for a non-league football club with 500 fans. Today, we are regularly attracting crowds ten times that size, with ambitions to go considerably further. Over the years, we have invested substantially in improving our home, increasing capacity to approximately 7,800. However, even with further investment to replace and expand the East and West Stands, the physical limitations of the site would restrict capacity to around 10,000 and the footprint would be unchanged. The reality is that our current stadium footprint is increasingly a constraint on our future.

"If we genuinely want to compete consistently at the upper end of League One and give ourselves the opportunity to establish Stevenage FC in the Championship, we must think differently.

"Over the Summer, we opened discussions with stakeholders to explore potential opportunities and sites for a new stadium-led development that becomes a significant community and commercial asset for Stevenage, bringing together sport, leisure, hospitality, retail, business and more, creating a destination that generates employment, attracts investment, and delivers well beyond matchdays. That search is now well under way.

"Other clubs are already recognising the opportunities presented by new stadium-led regeneration; Luton Town has commenced a new stadium development, while Cambridge United has recently outlined its own long-term ambitions for a similar project. Stevenage cannot be left behind and there are important questions to answer around location, funding, infrastructure and the wider needs of the town. However, I firmly believe both the ambition and the timing are right.

"We have always been a challenger club, taking on teams with greater resources and proving what can be achieved through hard work, smart investment and determination. Our next chapter should build on those foundations.

"But none of this means anything without our supporters. We want to compete at the highest level we can and to achieve that, we need the whole town behind us.

"We have achieved an enormous amount in our first 50 years. Let’s make the next 50 years even greater.”

Those clubs mentioned in the same local and regional area as Stevenage, Luton Town and Cambridge United, are themselves looking to expand.

Luton are further along in the process with The Hatters due to move out of their famous home, Kenilworth Road, and to the Power Court site in central Luton for the 2028-29 campaign. The new stadium is set to hold 25,000 as opposed to Kenilworth Road’s capacity of 12,056.

As for Cambridge, The U’s are exploring plans for a 15,000-capacity stadium in the east of the city as they seek to move away from the 7,937-capacity Abbey Stadium, which has been their home since 1932.

Stevenage have more or less been on an upward trajectory since their reformation in the 1970s and then kicking on in the 80s, with the club suffering just two relegations in their history; from Isthmian Division One in the 1987-88 campaign and then from League One in the 2013-14 season.

Having finished ninth, 14th and then sixth since returning to the third-tier in 2023; it is therefore now not unreasonable to suggest Stevenage will eventually likely batter down the door and get themselves into the second-tier, which would be an overachievement for many neutrals but one that similar sized clubs such as Lincoln City and Wycombe Wanderers have managed in the recent past.

The impressive off-field decision-making that has allowed for Stevenage to remain competitive is something Wallace also mentioned in his statement.

We rank all 24 League One stadiums by their ground's distance from the city or town, starting with the longest distance.

He said: “As the club celebrates its 50th anniversary, I believe this is the right moment to reflect on how far we have come and set out our ambitions for where we go next.

"We cannot predict what football will look like in 50 years. But we can, and absolutely should, plan for the next 10, 20 years and beyond.

"We have never been a club that stands still. Our philosophy has always been to build for the future, often ahead of where we are on the pitch. That approach has taken us from local non-league football to an established EFL club, competing nationally against clubs with significantly greater resources.

Over the past 15 years, we have transformed our football operation through sustained investment in our training grounds, stadium, Academy, and women’s team. More recently, we have expanded into new areas, including Rookies nursery and a new Padel facility, creating additional revenue streams that support the long-term future of the club.

"Pound for pound, I believe there are few clubs in English football that make better use of their resources than Stevenage FC, both on and off the pitch. Last season, we were the most efficient club in League One when measured by points achieved against playing budget, demonstrating what can be accomplished through intelligent investment and sound financial management.

"Our football recruitment strategy is a further example of that approach. We have built a model focused on identifying the right characters and talent, recognising untapped value. So far in 2026, we have generated more than £2.25 million in net player revenue while continuing to rebuild a competitive first-team squad and staff structure.”

An example would be that in the summer transfer window, manager Alex Revell had to deal with the sales of a couple of key players such as Filip Marschall and Jamie Reid, while Charlie Goode and Harvey White also departed.

Despite that, Stevenage continue to compete with their 1-0 defeat of Oxford United at the Kassam Stadium in the FA Cup first round at the weekend backing up a 2-2 draw with Leicester City away from home, leaving them six points off the play-off places with a steady but improving record of four wins, four draws and four defeats in their 12 League One matches thus far.

Despite the club remaining in a competitive spot on the pitch and clearly impressive work has been done to sustain their rise off the pitch, there has been a mixed response to the update from chairman Wallace.

Both Stevenage supporters and fans of other clubs, such as Huddersfield Town and Plymouth Argyle, have proffered the point that Stevenage don’t tend to sell out their current stadium and so a bigger one would surely just have the same issue and is therefore unnecessary.

Others, though, have acknowledged that the ambition of the club is exciting and the intentions of stadium regeneration is a further example of that.

Division and debate is a part of football and Stevenage may have to navigate some dissenters but Wallace and the club have made clear their plans to continue to push ‘Boro onto the next level.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

CAT puts century-old train tech to work in its biggest underground machine yet

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Diesel-electric locomotives have been barreling along America’s wide-open spaces for generations, and the century-old combination of highly efficient diesel engines spinning up generators to power high-torque electric motors has proven tough to beat. Now, Caterpillar is putting that century-old concept to work underground. Meet the CAT R3200 XE LHD.

One of the many hard-working machines moving chunks of Earth under your feet, an LHD (load, haul, and dump) machine is essentially a massive underground wheel loader designed to scoop up blasted rock, haul it through narrow mine tunnels, then dump it into trucks or ore passes. Built low and wide to fit in the tunnels, LHDs are workhorses of underground mining operations – and the new R3200 XE is CAT’s biggest yet, with a 25 ton payload and electric drive.

Before we get into the machine’s specs (the few that we have, anyway), I want to make sure understand that large-scale mining operations use obscene amounts of fuel, and a swing in site efficiency of just 1-2% can mean a huge difference in the bottom line – and fuel prices were up 27.4% (!) in August alone.

Keep the staggering weight of what that kind of fuel cost can mean to a fleet that buys fuel by the literal truckload as you read the rest of this.

Though not yet officially released by CAT, snippets of the company’s launch video have been leaking through dealers and trade journals, and they show the big machine powered by CAT’s newest 18.1 liter Tier 5 C18 diesel generator making 527 hp (~390 kW). That C18 reportedly sends electrons to the same electric drive system used in the company’s already successful 18-ton R2900 XE LHD.

In addition to a bigger generator and heftier payload, CAT says the upcoming R3200 XE LHD offers reduced cycle times versus the R2900 XE with 11% better speed on grade, plus reduced maintenance and repair costs thanks to design refinements for better durability, including redesigned axle. All told, that works out to 28% more tons per hour with 8% better fuel efficiency.

That efficiency is boosted by the same baked-in ADAS tech that’s made the smaller R2900 XE a favorite, and includes the latest version of CAT AutoDig and MineStar, ensuring that the machines are easy for experienced CAT operators to set up for remote, semi-autonomous, and even fully autonomous operation using Command for Underground.

We’ll post an update once CAT makes the full rundown of official specs public, which should be sooner rather than later. When that happens, we’ll see exactly how this big burrowing CAT stacks up against the competition from Sandvik, Epiroc, and Komatsu. In the meantime, let us know what you think of CAT’s newest electric drive machine in the comments.

RC Lens : Yannick Cahuzac déjà limogé !

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Yannick Cahuzac n'est plus l'entraîneur principal du RC Lens. Nommé il y a seulement deux mois sur le banc artésien, le technicien a été limogé de son poste ce lundi 9 novembre 2026 par sa direction après une série noire en championnat et en Ligue des champions.

Selon les informations du journal L'Equipe, le couperet est tombé pour Yannick Cahuzac. Réunis ce lundi soir, les dirigeants du RC Lens ont acté le départ de leur technicien au lendemain d'une nouvelle défaite à domicile contre l'OM (1-2) en Ligue 1, dimanche 8 novembre 2026. Ce revers devant le public lensois est survenu quatre jours après une claque historique concédée à Bollaert en Ligue des champions face à Côme (1-9), le mercredi 4 novembre 2026. Une humiliation aux conséquences immédiates. Contrairement à l'hypothèse d'un maintien au sein de l'organigramme, l'entraîneur ne sera pas rétrogradé dans le staff technique et quitte définitivement le club artésien.

L'expérience de Yannick Cahuzac aura tourné court dans le Pas-de-Calais. Promu entraîneur principal le 15 septembre 2026 en remplacement de Dino Toppmöller, après un revers initial à Monaco (1-2), il n'aura finalement dirigé que huit rencontres toutes compétitions confondues. Le bilan est famélique : trois victoires et cinq défaites en deux mois. Pourtant, les débuts avaient semblé prometteurs avec deux succès en Ligue 1 contre Lyon et Troyes, malgré une défaite face au Sporting CP en Ligue des champions. Toutefois, la dynamique s'est rapidement enrayée. Le RC Lens a concédé quatre défaites lors de ses cinq derniers matches, chutant lourdement sur la scène européenne contre Bruges (0-3) puis face à Côme (1-9), avant d'aligner deux revers consécutifs en championnat face à Lille (0-1) et Marseille.

Désormais en quête d'un nouvel élan, l'état-major lensois s'active en coulisses pour ouvrir le feuilleton de sa succession. Selon les informations de L'Équipe, les décideurs artésiens auraient trois noms en tête pour reprendre les rênes de l'équipe première. Parmi ces profils figurerait notamment... Pierre Sage, qui avait quitté le club cet été et qui se retrouverait aujourd'hui menacé à Crystal Palace. Une hypothèse encore loin d'être concrétisée. En attendant de connaître l'identité de son futur coach, le club nordiste doit préparer sa prochaine échéance. Le prochain match du RC Lens est prévu le samedi 21 novembre 2026 à 20h45 contre Toulouse à Bollaert, à l'occasion de la 11e journée de Ligue 1.

Your iPhone Duo Under Attack? Binance Flags Dangerous iOS Security Flaw

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Binance has warned Apple users about a dangerous iOS vulnerability that could expose crypto wallets, private keys, and login credentials. The flaw, called worse_query, allows malicious applications to access files normally isolated inside other apps. Binance warning identifies several affected iOS software versions and urges users to remove untrusted applications immediately.

The vulnerability threatens more than crypto wallets; apps storing sensitive information locally could also expose passwords, authentication data, and personal files. That makes the latest warning particularly concerning for iPhone users who rely on their devices for banking and crypto transactions.

A vulnerability (worse_query) affecting iOS 26.7.1, 27.0.1, and 27.2 allows malicious apps to read local files from other apps, putting seed phrases, private keys, and login credentials at risk across the entire ecosystem. This is an iOS-level issue, not unique to Binance.…

Binance says the problem affects iOS itself rather than its own wallet infrastructure. Users who suspect their devices have been compromised should create new wallets on clean devices and move their funds. The exchange also recommends resetting active login sessions and updating installed software.

The vulnerability exploits Apple’s application sandbox, a security mechanism designed to keep applications separated from one another. Normally, an application cannot freely access another application’s private files. This isolation protects sensitive information even when users install multiple applications on the same device.

This is where it gets dangerous: the worse_query exploit reportedly bypasses those restrictions. A malicious application could potentially access directories containing information belonging to other installed applications.

The researcher behind the public GitHub repository describes worse_query as a sandbox escape affecting iOS 26 and iOS 27. The project identifies affected releases spanning iOS 26.0 through 26.7.2 and iOS 27.2. Binance warns that exposed information could include cryptocurrency recovery phrases, private keys, and login credentials.

The actual danger depends partly on how individual applications store sensitive information. Wallets retaining recovery phrases or other credentials in accessible local files face particular risks.

Security researchers have also highlighted the importance of protecting plaintext wallet information. SlowMist’s security chief warned that the vulnerability could undermine assumptions about isolation between application containers.

Apple’s security update schedule adds another dimension to the Binance iOS warning. Apple’s security releases list iOS 27.2 and iOS 26.7.2 among its available software versions, both released on October 26, 2026.

However, Apple’s published security advisories do not explicitly identify worse_query by name. There is currently no confirmed public statement establishing that these updates fully resolve the specific sandbox escape.

A separate GitHub project implementing the exploit also reported on November 8 that Apple’s available software releases had not patched worse_query. This leaves users with an important gap between installing the latest available update and confirming that a particular vulnerability has been fixed.

Binance recommends installing the latest official iOS release and keeping all applications updated. It also urges users to remove applications from unknown sources, including manually signed IPA files.

For us, crypto holders, the safest response to suspected compromise goes beyond simply deleting suspicious software.

The latest iPhone warning follows several security incidents involving malicious applications and crypto credentials. In September, Binance warned users about FomoPeek, a third-party application whose versions 1.1 and 1.2 reportedly contained malicious code.

Security researchers found that the software could exploit iOS vulnerabilities to obtain elevated device privileges. Potentially exposed information included recovery phrases, private keys, login credentials, and other locally stored data.

More recently, CertiK highlighted the DarkSword exploit framework, which reportedly targeted cryptocurrency applications through compromised websites and vulnerable iPhones.

#CertiKInsight 🚨 A new version of DarkSword, an iOS exploit kit, is being used to target crypto wallets on unpatched iPhones. Simply opening a malicious site in Safari can be enough: no wallet connection, no signature needed. Here's what you need to know 🧵

These incidents involve separate attack methods. Nevertheless, they demonstrate why smartphone security has become increasingly important for crypto holders.

The Binance iOS warning highlights a fundamental problem with self-custody: protecting a recovery phrase means protecting every place where that information might be stored.

Sisters in Sweat: how a former Spurs player got 20,000 Indian women into sport

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A dinner-party question, a Sunday kickabout and an idea that snowballed. Nine years on, Tanvie Hans and Swetha Subbiah have taken sporting sisterhood to 10 cities

On a breezy evening in 2017, as conversations flowed and so did the drinks at a dinner party, Tanvie Hans was asked an all-too-familiar question at the table: “Hey, can you teach me how to play football?”

For the Indian-born British footballer, who played professionally in England, it was an easy yes.

After a little planning and some back-and-forth messages 17 women – mostly friends and acquaintances – turned up for Hans’ first football lesson on a Sunday morning in the southern Indian metropolis of Bengaluru, eager to discover the joy of the sport.

Nine years on from that impromptu football session, the group has grown into a community of 20,000 women across India. They may not be sisters by blood, but they are the Sisters in Sweat. Co-founded by Hans and the performance coach Swetha Subbiah, Sisters in Sweat is India’s largest women-first sports community, built to help women move more, play more and connect through a variety of sports.

From Chandigarh in the north to Chennai in the south, the initiative now spans 10 Indian cities, with football among its most popular offerings. “It wasn’t something that Swetha and I sat down to plan or even recognised was a gap in the market,” Hans, a former Tottenham and Fulham midfielder, tells Moving the Goalposts.

“But the moment we started these sessions they became successful and the community kept growing organically. That’s when we recognised that we were catering to an audience that is not being catered to in this way. And who better to do it than two women who are experts in the field?”

At Sisters in Sweat, coach-led football sessions are conducted two to three times a week, with participants free to choose between beginner and intermediate sessions, depending on their level of ability. The entry fee starts at about 300 Indian rupees – just under £2.50 – and women of all ages are welcome. You could be trying football for the first time, getting back to it or simply looking for a fun way to stay active.

The format is pretty simple, and the same one Hans and Subbiah used at their first gathering in 2017. The session begins with a quick huddle; everyone introduces themselves and the coach explains what to expect in the next 90 minutes. A warm-up follows before partner or group drills help break the ice within the group, which often features new faces every week. There is music playing in the background to create a celebratory mood and the session ends with a quick and easy 30-minute game of football.

A first-time participant often leaves with a smile, having ticked off a workout while meeting a bunch of strangers who they can now call friends. “You feel like, ‘Oh, I can actually play football and want to get better. I can’t wait to come back for the next session’,” Hans says. “Honestly, just being at the session opens doors for connection. It happens very naturally, and by the end of 90 minutes, you feel you belong here.”

Beyond nurturing new connections, Sisters in Sweat offers women something increasingly rare in India’s densely populated tier 1 cities: access to third spaces where they can show up and move their bodies without fear of judgment or safety concerns. With India’s fitness market projected to grow from $12.3bn (£9.2bn) in 2024 to $23.2bn by 2030 – an 88.6% increase over six years – Sisters in Sweat’s growth and expansion reflect the broader industry trend.

“My cousin, who is from the UK, was actively involved in our Sisters in Sweat sessions while she was in India on holiday. But after she returned to the UK, she wasn’t able to find a community like that,” Subbiah says. “I have heard the same thing from friends in Singapore and other countries. So, I think we, in many ways, have been the first movers to identify this.”

No topic is too small or too big for us to cover as we deliver a twice-weekly roundup of the wonderful world of women’s football

At the heart of Sisters in Sweat’s aim is to fill the gaping hole left by Indian women dropping out of sport, especially after school or college. In the country’s deeply patriarchal social structure, many women are often discouraged from venturing outdoors alone or playing sport recreationally. For many, having the freedom to stay out late at night is a distant dream or a luxury, at best.

“Once a woman leaves her role as a student, she moves on to her next role in life, which is typically becoming a wife,” Subbiah, who has been a Nike coach for over a decade, says. “Then she takes on all the household duties. Add a kid into the mix, and her personal time is minimised.

“There is also immense pressure on a lot of Indian women not to go out or play outdoors because they could catch a tan and look less appealing. A lot of the work we’ve done is to push past these social constructs and say, “Get a tan, it’s beautiful. Come out and play with us, you’ll feel great.”

Valve Is Improving The Data About How Well A Game Will Run On Your Steam Deck

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Steam has delivered a refresh of its store page for Steam Deck owners, allowing people to see the average framerate a game will run at on the handheld device based on the performance data of other players.

While games often arrive as verified to work on Steam Deck, this doesn’t always translate to workingÂ well. To let players know just how well a game will perform on Valve’s portable PC, others who have opted in to share their data will contribute to a consensus on possible framerates.

Under the additional info for Steam Deck compatibility on the game page, a new note will display the average framerate. It will also be possible to expand this data further by looking at the histogram, which will qualify the average into a graph that not only displays how common it is for a game to run at that framerate, but also how often the game hitches or stutters for players.

According to Valve, although the current information relies on standardized test criteria, the denominations âmay not match every customerâs bar for acceptable gameplay.â The company is also planning to expand this feature beyond Steam Deck in order to provide framerate information for more devices. The feature is already live for games with enough data points to provide an average. For example, right now Final Fantasy VII Rebirth shows that Steam Deck OLED players run the game at an average of 37 frames per second, while those playing Hades II are seeing 88 FPS on the same hardware.

With portable PC devices like Steam Deck, ROG Ally and even the Ayn Thor becoming more standard for players who donât need the most expensive hardware, seeing how a game will perform with more specificity is only a good thing. It doesnât hurt that Steam are planning to expand the Steam Deck lineup in future, and having such features available only makes the hardware more accessible for those less accustomed to PC gaming.

A review of ‘Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza | November 10, 2026

Was today’s modern solar industry inspired by off-grid California-living, marijuana-growing, Grateful Dead-loving hippies? That’s the main question explored in “Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV,” a new documentary that recently premiered in Grass Valley, California.

The entertaining documentary traces the history, colorful characters, off-grid homeowners, solar educators and the entrepreneurs who embraced solar photovoltaic technology for vastly different reasons.

Shot on a shoestring budget with volunteers, “Solar Roots” may not have the polished production of a Hollywood documentary, but it is an untold story about the people and early solar and technology that led to the modern solar industry.

Since 2009, when I first started in the solar industry, I had heard jokes and anecdotes about the marijuana-growing hippies who sparked the residential solar industry. Until “Solar Roots,” no one had ever set up a camera to talk to those early solar pioneers and document why rural America became a hub of off-grid solar innovation.

Enter Jeff Spies, a longtime solar veteran and the documentary’s executive producer. In 2015, Spies organized a weekend party for the off-grid solar pioneers of Northern California, where he started his career in the mid-2000s. After hearing so many colorful stories about why these rural homesteaders bought $14/watt solar panels and started new solar installation and distribution businesses, Spies enlisted another solar industry veteran, Jason Vetterli, to travel to Southern California, Arizona, Oregon, Colorado and Wisconsin to capture these unique and personal stories on video.

This isn’t the first documentary about the solar industry or its challenges, and to be clear, Vetterli and Spies’ narrative is not about creating “the solar biz.” Truly, it’s about a particular tribe of solar early adopters. Through interviews, we learn about the 1970s, ’80s and ’90s counterculture movement and the people who stumbled into solar businesses out of necessity and good timing.

Listening to these stories, you quickly understand that none of these solar pioneers intended to get into the solar business to make millions or solve climate change. Their real motivations were tied to maintaining their independence from cities while having enough electricity to keep the lights on and the music playing while raising a family. Beyond the home applications, solar became a new water-pumping solution that supported the pioneers’ cash-only “agricultural” businesses.

Vetterli sets the film’s tone and theme with the first scene. Instead of fading in to a utility solar farm or suburban residential roofs, the film opens with a young hippie using a wire to connect a single 35-watt ARCO solar panel to a car battery that powers an old cassette boombox. He then inserts a Grateful Dead tape and presses play.

The Grateful Dead-inspired musical score, composed by Midnight North, a band whose members include another solar veteran, TJ Kanczuzewski, appropriately plays under the rest of the film’s narrative and interviews.

After a brief solar technology history lesson, Spies interviews the solar pioneers about the roots of their solar PV fascination, how they came to realize that solar could grow and impact their lives and their off-grid movement.

This realization wouldn’t have happened without the back-to-the-land movement. Buying land was inexpensive but getting a utility to build a power line to DIY houses in rural California cost millions. While living without electricity, we learn about their lives before and after PV.

Before PV, the pioneers had to jerry-rig their pickup trucks’ batteries to listen to music and use dangerous kerosene lanterns for light. Gasoline generators were loud, costly, required maintenance and were contrary to the quiet, remote lifestyle they wanted. After PV (and lead-acid batteries), the solar pioneers could enjoy light, music, small DC refrigerators for cold beer and fresh food, 12-volt TVs and other DC devices that were designed for camping.

After implementing solar PV into their own homesteads, the pioneers wanted to share their find with their neighbors. It was that desire to share the off-grid solar PV love that led to the early solar distributor businesses, publications and training centers — not a desire to make millions.

As businesses grew, cultures clashed. We hear the story of when corporate solar salespeople traveled to Humboldt County in three-piece suits to meet their best customers who are wearing T-shirts and jeans and carry the scent and cash of a “weedpreneur.”

While the majority of the film is about the people who wanted an off-grid lifestyle, Vetterli and Spies also interview the late 1990s PV code and policy pioneers. These solar people also wore suit, but they also saw grid-tied potential to make solar mainstream.

“Solar Roots” does a great job covering this off-grid to grid-tied transition, but the film only covers a fraction of the stories and people who inspired the multi-gigawatt and global solar industry that exists today, and that’s O.K. A single two-hour documentary could never cover all of the world’s solar pioneers, but we’re fortunate to have met these particular solar people. They may not have started the residential solar industry, but they certainly contributed to it.

Spies and Vetterli plan to release the entire documentary online in the coming months and have set up a mailing list at www.solar-roots.com for people to receive updates.

Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza is a former film and TV writer (Stargate SG-1, Dharma & Greg, The Dead Zone) and current chief solar public relations geek at UnThink Solar, a solar PR and communications firm.

Sunrise Scramble

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

I bring back a classic backpacking mistake in time for sunrise. I enjoy views of the Hudson Valley and camp above some noisy animals.

Sunrise Mountain, I couldn’t get it out of my head. I hiked over something called Sunrise Mountain and couldn’t get myself up to see a sunrise there. It was a good time of year for sunrises, the nights were long, allowing ample time for sleep before getting up and finding a place to catch the sun as it breaks over the horizon. I had gotten up early yesterday morning and had a glimpse of the pre-sunrise sky on my drive into town. It was exquisite. Even though time had moved back an hour and sunrise would now be at 6:30AM instead of 7:30AM, I knew that my body clock was more aligned to the cycle of light and dark than the numbers on my phone’s lock screen. I could wake up at 5:40AM, get myself together, and hike up the 1.1 miles the east-facing viewpoint south of Culver’s Gap. Part of me thought I was nuts, but nonetheless I set an alarm for 5:40AM and dozed off to sleep.

Except I didn’t set an alarm for 5:40AM. Like something out of an episode of Seinfeld I set the alarm for 5:40PM. Lucky for me I have my “punctuality anxiety” system to fall back, which rarely fails to deliver. I was up before the alarm and could wiggle enough that I felt I could get myself up and actually do this. I pulled myself together, decided it would be best to pack my breakfast and have it on trail to make time, and was heading toward the trail at 5:50AM. I had strapped on my headlamp, which I hadn’t used since May, to light up the trail, though after about 10 minutes it was easy enough to see anyway. I did notice during that 10 minutes that it was dimming itself, I made a note to look into this since I wouldn’t want that happening when I really needed it.

I reached the viewpoint by 6:20AM, which is when I noticed that I forgot my water bottle in rush to leave the van. I had flashbacks to one of my first days in Pennsylvania when I managed to forget a series of critical items during a calamitous day. I ended up hiking the better part of 2 days without the use of my water bottle. I knew I couldn’t afford the time to hike back to the van and continue my planned itinerary, which called for at least 27 miles. I began coming up with alternative ideas while the sun peered over the horizon, smirking at my classic blunder.

Sunrise with Culver Lake in the foreground.

Sunrise with Culver Lake in the foreground.

Having fun with light settings at sunrise.

The sun is always ready for its close-up.

As I was adequately hydrated and could filter water into my cold-soaker if I really needed it, I decided to keep my momentum and continue hiking south. The trail up on the ridge in the Delaware Water Gap National Recreation Area was decidedly easy. There were frequent views into the valley and several white tail deer roaming the area. I used the mild conditions to my advantage and hiked 11 miles to a viewpoint above Fairview Lake. I turned around and used the return leg to take the alternate route around Crater Lake (not the national park). This allowed me to get some closer pictures of the lake and make use of some side trails parallel to the AT. After that it was the AT the rest of the way back to the van at Culver’s Gap.

Culver Lake, from roughly where I took pictures of the sunrise this morning.

I was finally able to have my breakfast having survived the trek without water. My trail snack stood in for a breakfast and kept my energy levels up. From Culver’s Gap I drove to the Appalachian Trail crossing on Millbrook Road. It turned out to be a small trailhead that didn’t quite look suitable to leave a vehicle overnight, but I decided to go for it anyway. My motivation was catching the sunset. When I pieced together an alternate route, I knew I’d be leaving a gap between Millbrook Road and the point where I turned around earlier in the day. I be hiking south since that is where the better view points and tent spots were. I only had to hike about 2 miles to them, which is why I decided to hike 22 miles in the first out and back stint. Tomorrow I’d continue the journey south all the way to the Dunnfield Creek Trailhead near the Delaware River, then turn around, pass the van, and continue north to the point where I turned around. It was the best way to split the miles between the two days and still work in a night on the trail with a sunset and potentially sunrise.

I set off from Millbrook Road and stopped by the Rattlesnack Spring to collect 2 liters of water for the night. From there it was a fairly mild hike up to the Catfish Fire Tower. I could see a ranger positioned high above in the tower and an old-school forest service truck parked at the base of the tower.

Past the tower the viewpoints and tent spots came fast and furious. I was holding out for one with a sunset view, but all were positioned on the east side of the ridge. Eventually I settled for one of those spots that did have an incredible view of the valley. Down below, dogs were barking and a rooster was crowing. Occasionally a passing plane drowned them out. Despite the noise I was content with my spot because you just can’t beat being perched on a cliff with a front row seat to a sunrise.

View of the Hudson Valley from the trail.

The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

To learn more, please visit the About This Site page.

I’m See You Tomorrow (SYT), a 30-something hiker who loves hiking just for the thrill of seeing things that most people don’t. I have no problem eating the same things every day and, for me, over-analyzing everything is half the fun (sometimes more). I am new to backpacking but that won’t stop me from taking on an unorthodox hike of the Appalachian Trail in 2026. It’s not quite a yo-yo, but I have aspirations of hiking the entire trail twice in one year. For the rest of the story you’ll just have to start reading my blog!

Review: Apple's M6 MacBook Pro is good, but it's missing all the exciting stuff

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Per usual, the basic MacBook Pro lags behind the flagships where it counts.

I’ve written more than once about the weird positioning of Apple’s entry-level 14-inch MacBook Pro, which has always occupied an uncomfortable perch between the MacBook Air and the higher-end MacBook Pros. The best versions of this laptop, like last year’s M5 refresh, come with the same basic design and external features of the higher-end MacBook Pros, just with a more modest processor and a lower ceiling for specs like RAM and storage.

The lesser versions stick with older designs or other compromises, reserving Apple’s most updated designs and screen technologies for the more expensive high-end models. Recall the 13-inch M1 and M2 MacBook Pros, which stuck with Apple’s 2016-era MacBook Pro designs for everything, up to and including the now-abandoned Touch Bar. (If you’re someone at Apple maintaining Touch Bar support into the 2030s for the sake of these two laptop models, I’d like to hear from you. Not for a story! I’d just like to know how you are.)

This year’s entry-level MacBook Pro mostly falls into that latter category. It does get an Apple M6 chip, and the M6 is great—rumors suggest the M6 won’t get Pro, Max, or Ultra configurations, so there are some fringe benefits here that you can’t get in the top-tier MacBook Pros. But it uses the same MacBook Pro design Apple has been using since 2021. There’s no touchscreen, no OLED panel, no Dynamic Island, no half-pound weight reduction, no glowing Apple logo on the lid.

In other words, it has very little of what makes the “PROLED XDR” MacBook Pros interesting, despite starting at an objectively very high starting price of $1,999. This is $700 more than the basic M1 MacBook Pro from 2020 and $400 more than the starting price of the M5 MacBook Pro from a year ago (though that model also got a mandatory 1TB storage upgrade and a $400 price hike when Apple raised most of its prices back in June).

That doesn’t mean there’s no constituency for the base-model MacBook Pro, which remains the cheapest way to get a 120 Hz ProMotion display or the anti-glare nano-texture display finish from one of Apple’s laptops. I just wish paying this kind of money was enough to get you Apple’s best technology.

There’s nothing new here compared to the M5 MacBook Pro, but just in case it’s been a few years, let’s run down the basics and go over what makes this laptop different from the MacBook Air.

Though it’s now a few years old, this iteration of the MacBook Pro design still looks and feels good, particularly with a nano-texture display installed. The laptop looks and feels (and is) thicker and heavier than either the 13- or 15-inch MacBook Air, but it also comes with more ports. In addition to the MagSafe port, a pair of Thunderbolt 4 ports, and a headphone jack on the laptop’s left side, you also get a dedicated HDMI port, another Thunderbolt 4 port, and an SD card reader on the right side.

For those who use USB-C chargers, it’s nice to be able to plug the laptop in to charge it from either side. For everyone else, it’s just nice to have more ports and need fewer dongles.

The MacBook Pro keyboard and trackpad are broadly pretty comparable to the Air’s, though if pressed, I’d say the Pro’s feels ever-so-slightly crisper and firmer to type on.

At 14.2 inches, the MacBook Pro’s screen sits between the 13.6- and 15.3-inch MacBook Air, though in practice it feels closer to the 13. And while it’s not OLED, it still comes with some notable upgrades to the Air’s regular old 60 Hz IPS laptop screens. These include a mini LED display panel with local dimming support that helps to improve contrast and lower black levels, a higher resolution than either MacBook Air display, a maximum SDR brightness of 1,000 nits and HDR brightness of up to 1,600 nits, the $150 nano-texture matte display upgrade, and, of course, the 120 Hz ProMotion refresh rate.

This is no longer a cutting-edge screen within Apple’s lineup, but it’s still very nice. It’s the biggest selling point for the basic MacBook Pro—aside from the M6, though the Air will presumably get that chip eventually, as it usually does. More on that in a bit.

But now that Apple is using OLED touchscreens in its flagship laptops, we’ll hopefully see all these models benefit from the gradual technology trickle-down that we generally see in Apple products. Just as the older iPhone eventually inherited OLED panels and ProMotion support from the iPhone Pro, I hope that in the next generation or two, we see OLED/touch come to this baseline MacBook Pro and ProMotion and/or nano-texture displays come to the MacBook Air. At least, I’d like to see those things if Apple can add them without a big price hike.

Apple’s M6 debuted in the new Mac mini a couple of months back, and it performs about the same in the M6 MacBook Pro (and in the M6 iMac) as it did there.

As a recap, the M6 is a fairly major refresh for Apple’s lowest-end chip. Both the M4 and the M5 used a mix of four high-performance CPU cores and six high-efficiency CPU cores (up from four of each in the M1/M2/M3 generations), and a maximum of 10 GPU cores (M2 and M3 also had 10, M1 maxed out at 8). And all Apple Silicon chips up until the M6 shipped with a 16-core Neural Engine NPU.

The M6 gets more CPU cores: two top-performance super cores, four performance cores, and six high-efficiency cores, for a total of 12. The GPU core count also increases to 12. And the Neural Engine is doubled, for a total of 32 NPU cores. RAM will still be the biggest limitation for the kinds of models that run well on an M6, but tasks that do engage the NPU should feel faster.

The RAM configurations on offer haven’t changed: 16GB is still the base, with 24GB and 32GB upgrade options. For the 16GB config, memory bandwidth is locked at 153 GB/s, the same as in the M5, but the 24GB and 32GB versions get a bump to 170 GB/s.

That brings us to our benchmark charts. The MacBook Pro review unit Apple sent ships with 16GB of RAM, down from the 24GB of RAM in the M6 Mac mini. That means the system is working with 90 percent of the memory bandwidth the Mac mini has; in practice, you mostly see this manifest as slightly lower graphics/gaming benchmarks, but they’re usually not enough to move the topline more than a couple of percentage points.

The chip is still a solid upgrade from the M5. Compared to last year’s M5 MacBook Pro, this year’s 16GB version offers around 7 percent and 15 percent better single- and multi-core CPU performance and between 20 and 30 percent better GPU performance, depending on the test.

As usual, Apple’s year-over-year performance upgrades add up when you’re jumping multiple generations at once. Compared to the M3 MacBook Pro, the M6 version offers around 30 percent better single-core CPU performance and between 40 and 60 percent faster multi-core CPU and graphics performance. So if you’re still using an M1 or M2 MacBook Air or Pro, you’re in for a sizable upgrade you should notice in day-to-day use. Compared to an M4 or M5, it will feel faster, but likely not staggeringly so.

As with the Mac mini, the basic MacBook Pro getting an M6-generation chip while the rest of the lineup sticks with the M5 means there are some specific ways in which this cheaper Mac is faster than the more expensive versions. While the M5 Pro’s higher core count definitely swamps the M6 for multi-threaded CPU work, the M6 still delivers Apple’s best single-core CPU performance, as well as the larger Neural Engine.

If and when the MacBook Air gets an M6, the MacBook Pro should still be the faster system, especially for sustained workloads. The MacBook Pro and Mac mini include active cooling fans, while the MacBook Air is fanless—the Airs have historically performed a little worse because they throttle their performance more quickly to keep temperatures in check.

Finally, a word on power usage. When I reviewed the M6 Mac mini, Apple’s built-in powermetrics tool wasn’t working with the M6 yet—it would run, but wouldn’t give any actual numbers. That’s been fixed since then, and the good news is that the power usage numbers are very boring. Under load, the M6 is faster than the M5 while consuming the same amount of power, which means it’s more power-efficient.

It’s an exciting year for the MacBook Pro! Just not for this MacBook Pro.

That’s to be expected, I suppose, since this MacBook Pro tier didn’t get the 2021-era design updates until the M3 version was released in 2023. I would not be surprised if we were left waiting at least two years for an OLED touchscreen to trickle down to this laptop.

After all, the OLED MacBook Pros haven’t even fully replaced the non-OLED versions of the M5 Pro and M5 Max laptops. I’d expect the next-gen versions of those laptops to bring the OLED touchscreens down to those price points and eliminate the non-OLED versions, and then for the basic MacBook Pro to get the new screens sometime after that. But that’s assuming some kind of normal-ish technology progression, and the times are still anything but normal, mostly thanks to the ongoing RAM and storage shortages.

The long and short of it is that this isn’t a bad laptop, but it is an awkwardly positioned one. This is always true, but this year more than usual. This laptop’s target buyer is “someone who is OK with MacBook Air-ish performance, but with more ports and the best-possible screen,” and the M6 version no longer gets Apple’s best-possible screen. Especially if you don’t need the now-mandatory 1TB storage allotment, it’s hard to look at the 13- or 15-inch MacBook Air, then look at this, and determine that you’re getting $500 to $700 worth of upgrades.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

easyJet, Ryanair & Wizz Air Cancel 25 Routes From London: See Full List Now

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

easyJet, Ryanair, and Wizz Air play a critical role in short-haul services from London-area airports. In 2026, OAG data shows that the trio has nearly one in two such departures from the UK capital (47%).

2026 is the first year that the three airlines have had more than 200,000 departures from London. 200,295 are available, which equates to an average of 549 daily outbound flights. This hides big variation, with daily activity varying from just 94 flights on Christmas Day (when Ryanair won't operate) to a high of 633 on May 29.

Schedule analysis shows that easyJet, Ryanair, and Wizz Air have collectively cut 25 routes from London recently. They all operated sometime between January and October 2026 but have no flights scheduled thereafter, even through 2027. 13 ended in September or October.

UK-headquartered low-cost carrier easyJet, whose board has agreed to a takeover by a US private equity firm, has ended a fifth of the 25 links. They include one market from London Gatwick, which is the UK's second-busiest airport and overwhelmingly the airline's most-served airport for flights across its entire network. easyJet is the second-largest short-haul operator from London, after British Airways.

End Month (January-October 2026 Only; No Flights Scheduled For 2027)

Years ago. Ended in 2009 but returned in 2024

Originally in 2021 only. Returned in 2026

easyJet reintroduced service between Gatwick and Newquay in June 2026, although flights ended two months later. The route, which covered 188 nautical miles (348 km), was served three times a week. easyJet returned after Isles of Scilly SkyBus pulled out, having not secured Public Service Obligation funding. SkyBus entered the market after Eastern Airways ceased to exist. Now Gatwick-Newquay has no flights at all.

While easyJet serves Gatwick, Luton, Southend, and Stansted, Ryanair's Boeing 737s operate from Gatwick, Luton, and Stansted. However, the ultra-low-cost carrier previously flew to Southend (where it had a base there) but pulled out in 2021. Unsurprisingly, Stansted is very much Ryanair's most-served airport across its full network of 230+ airports. This is primarily why most of the eliminated routes are from the Essex airport.

End Month (January-October 2026 Only; No Flights Scheduled For 2027)

Cuts in the examined period include both Bodrum and Dalaman in Turkey, as the airline has entirely pulled out of the country. After long seeing Ryanair's jets, Rodez has now been removed from Ryanair's network, but Toulouse isn't too far away. Salerno, near the Amalfi Coast, reopened for commercial service in 2024 after a 12-year absence. Ryanair has now ended all international flights there, but continues one domestic route. Naples is not far away.

Some of the routes have already started and some are starting in the next couple of days.

Wizz Air serves Gatwick and Luton, with bases at both airports. Just as easyJet's number one airport is Gatwick and Ryanair's is Stansted, Wizz Air's most-served airport across its entire network is Luton. However, the latest schedule information shows that it'll be overtaken by Rome Fiumicino next summer. Until 2025, Gatwick was in the top ten list. But due to many cuts and other changes, it is 31st in 2026 and will fall to 40th in the first ten months of 2027.

End Month (January-October 2026 Only; No Flights Scheduled For 2027)

Luton to Vienna (entirely pulled out of Vienna)

Gatwick to Medina (entirely pulled out of Medina)

Gatwick to Istanbul Airport (Luton to Istanbul Airport ended in early November, which is outside the examined period but worth a note)

Predictably, Wizz Air has cut more routes from the UK capital than easyJet and Ryanair combined. Perhaps the most notable eliminated links were to the Saudi Arabian cities of Jeddah and Medina. Served on the Airbus A321XLR, flights ran daily to both cities and had a maximum block time of nearly seven hours.

Ex-Burnley, West Brom & Southampton star Jay Rodriguez names best-ever teammate

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Jay Rodriguez announced his retirement last week, leaving many reminiscing over his time at Southampton, where he played a big part in their mini-Golden era in the early 2010s.

The 37-year-old scored goals wherever he went, whether that was at hometown club Burnley, West Brom or Southampton, but his best scoring season in the Premier League came at St Mary's, where he was one of the talismanic players under Mauricio Pochettino in the 2013/14 campaign.

Saints finished eighth that year, and while the Argentine left the club and numerous key players followed suit, they laid the foundations for numerous years on the fringes of, or actually getting into, Europe, and a run to the EFL Cup final in 2017.

Rodriguez scored 15 times that season, partnering with Rickie Lambert, who netted 13, with the likes of Morgan Schneiderlin, Jack Cork and a young James Ward-Prowse breaking through behind them.

But it was someone else in that Saints side whom the former striker proudly states is the best player he ever played with.

Rodriguez appeared on the 'I Had Trials Once' podcast last week to talk through his career, and spoke with Gaz Seddon and Gary Stopforth about that Southampton side, of which he made his solitary England appearance while playing in.

The trio ran through some of the players in that squad, including Nathaniel Clyne, Luke Shaw and Jose Fonte, alongside some of the names mentioned above, and Rodriguez was then asked if any of those stood out as "top" players while playing with them.

"Lallana," the ex-Saints man said, pretty easily. "I always say it in everything; he's the best I've ever played with.

Collect every point to win the round. Don’t let Neil Warnock catch you.

Arrow keys or WASD to move. P to pause, M to mute.

Swipe on the pitch or use the pad to move.

"Everything, technical, tactical, as a captain, he'd kill you if you weren't working hard in training, because he'd be working to his maximum. He's really passionate; he loves football."

Lallana was part of the Saints class who departed to 'bigger' things after that eighth-placed finish in the Premier League in 2013/14. He, Lambert and Dejan Lovren all left for Liverpool, while Luke Shaw went to Manchester United and Calum Chambers signed for Arsenal.

There's little surprise in Rodriguez stating that Lallana was the best footballer he'd ever played with, as the now-retired 38-year-old oozed class all the way from his time in League One with Southampton to the Premier League at Liverpool.

The midfielder was one of the driving forces in Saints' double promotion under Nigel Adkins, scoring 23 and assisting 15 across both League One campaigns and then registering 11 goals and 10 assists in their Championship promotion term in 2011/12.

Even the Premier League didn't seem too large a step-up, and when he moved to Anfield in 2014, he was one of the more important players in the early Jürgen Klopp days after the German boss became Liverpool manager in 2015.

Lallana netted eight and assisted seven in the 2016/17 Premier League campaign, but a hamstring injury the following year saw him miss 31 games. Unfortunately, he could never really get a lengthy run without breaking down after that as a Liverpool player.

Do you think Adam Lallana could have been a major player in Liverpool's recent golden era if not for injury? Let us know in the comments.

At Brighton and Hove Albion, he never played more than 1,600 league minutes in each of his four years there, with his minutes being managed even when he was fit to play, and he retired after a year back at Southampton during their most recent Premier League campaign in 2024/25.

Nearly a decade on from that initial hamstring injury, questions are still being asked regarding what his career could have looked like if he had remained healthy while Liverpool were starting to find their feet under Klopp, en route to becoming one of the best clubs in the World.

Rodriguez retires having been one of the more underrated goalscorers in the EFL over the 14 or so years he was in the limelight.

More often than not, he'd go under the radar towards a double-digit scoring campaign regardless of whether he was in the Championship or the Premier League, with his best year coming at West Brom in 2018/19, where both he and strike partner Dwight Gayle netted over 20 each for a Baggies side who finished in the play-offs.

Even back in 2022/23, a then-33-year-old Rodriguez netted ten for the Vincent Kompany-led Burnley side en route to the Championship title. From then, injuries and a place further down the pecking order stopped him from making much of an impact, both at Turf Moor and then at Wrexham.

Now the boots are hung up, it's fun to look back on some of the squads he was part of, but no doubt that early Southampton side in the Premier League with Adam Lallana pulling the strings in the midfield was the most entertaining.

This Special Type of ‘Bathing’ May Have Unexpected Immune Benefits

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It’s gotten especially popular in the past few years.

Sitting in a sauna is a nice way to unwind, whether you're starting your day or coming down post-workout. But while it can feel good to lounge in the heat to decompress, research suggests you might be doing your immune system a solid in the process, too.

The study, which was published in the journal Temperature, found that hanging (or ‘bathing,’ as it’s called) in the sauna for 30 minutes sparks a unique chain reaction in the body that could support your health. Scientists haven't quite figured out what these changes mean when it comes to measurable impact on immunity, but doctors say this is definitely one more thing to mull over while you're getting your sauna on. Here's why.

Meet the experts: Jari Laukkanen, MD, PhD, study co-author and professor at the University of Eastern Finland; Eric Ascher, DO, family medicine physician at Northwell’s Lenox Hill Hospital; and Joseph J. Ciotola, MD, an orthopedic surgeon at Baltimore's Mercy Medical Center

For the study, researchers recruited 51 adults with an average age of 50. Scientists took blood samples before asking participants to sit in a 163-degree sauna for 30 minutes. The participants gave blood samples again right after the sauna session and again 30 minutes later.

The researchers found that there was a temporary increase in every type of circulating white blood cell they measured, including immune cells called neutrophils and lymphocytes. Neutrophils are usually the first defenders to respond to an infection and lymphocytes recognize specific threats, make antibodies, and help coordinate immune responses. The increase only lasted for 30 minutes—the neutrophil and lymphocyte counts returned to baseline after that.

"We have studied sauna bathing and cardiovascular health for many years, and our previous studies have shown interesting associations with better health outcomes," says Jari Laukkanen, MD, PhD, study co-author and professor at the University of Eastern Finland. "We wanted to understand more about the biological mechanisms behind these findings, including possible effects on the immune system."

Again, the researchers simply found that this jump in certain white blood cells happens when someone sits in a sauna—they didn't figure out why it happens or what it means. Still, experts have a few theories.

One is that sauna bathing presents a sudden physical challenge for the body, according to Eric Ascher, DO, a family medicine physician at Northwell’s Lenox Hill Hospital. "This heat stress wakes up your nervous system, causing white blood cells stored in different parts of your body to rush into your bloodstream," he says.

This is similar to what happens in the body when you do a cold plunge, says Joseph J. Ciotola, MD, an orthopedic surgeon at Baltimore's Mercy Medical Center. “It’s just hormetic stress,” he adds, referring to the process where short stressors can trigger a positive adaptive response in the body and make you stronger.

The release of these immune cells is also similar to what happens during physical exercise, Laukkanen says.

As for what this means for the body, that's still being explored. "White blood cells are important for immune defense and surveillance," Laukkanen says. "Their temporary mobilization may help the body respond to physiological challenges."

But Laukkanen also says that it's not clear yet what this means for immune health. "Our study measured acute responses after one sauna session, and we cannot yet say whether these changes improve long-term immunity or reduce the risk of infections," he says. "More research is needed."

You do more than just sweat in a sauna. "Saunas make your heart beat faster, and your blood vessels open up, which is very similar to the benefits you get from taking a brisk walk or going for a light run," Ascher says. "Because of this, regular sauna use has been linked to lower blood pressure, a healthier heart, and a lower risk of getting certain long-term diseases." (Still, he stresses that there's no data now to suggest that sauna use will replace the need for medication or exercise.)

Sauna use can also help people relax, recover from stress, and support overall well-being, Laukkanen says. "Sauna can be a pleasant part of a healthy lifestyle, alongside regular physical activity," he adds.

Of course, not everyone should hop in the sauna. "Because the intense heat puts a lot of stress on your heart and body, anyone with serious heart problems, very low blood pressure, or severe dizziness should stay out of saunas," Ascher says. Pregnant women and older adults may also want to steer clear.

But if you already like hanging in the sauna, it's nice to know you might be doing your immune system a solid while you kick back and enjoy.

Carina Hsieh, MPH, is the deputy features editor of Women’s Health. She has more than a decade’s worth of experience working in media and has covered everything from beauty, fashion, travel, lifestyle, pets, to health.

She began her career as an intern in the fashion closet at Cosmopolitan where she worked her way up to Senior Sex & Relationships Editor. While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

Jensen Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy further complicates Nvidia’s China problem

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's admission on Sunday to an extramarital affair with alleged Chinese spy Alice Huang, the chipmaker's relationship to China is about to come under intense scrutiny in Washington, D.C.

Since October 2022, when the Biden administration announced sweeping controls to limit China's ability to obtain advanced U.S. chips, Nvidia has seen its business diminish in the world's second-largest economy. Months after an even stricter set of rules was implemented by the Trump administration, Huang said in October 2025, "We are 100% out of China."

The recent allegations against Huang, while throwing the chipmaker into crisis, have also brought attention to the Nvidia CEO's potential influence over U.S. policy on chip sales to China, where its business once flourished. Of particular interest is whether restricted Nvidia chips have reached Chinese companies through customers like Singapore-based Megaspeed, the company linked to Alice Huang.

The New York Times first reported that the Chinese businesswoman was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, according to people briefed on the intelligence circulated in a memo among the Trump administration in the spring. China has called the allegation a smear.

Jensen Huang has been closely aligned with President Donald Trump during his second administration, helping advise the White House on artificial intelligence safety regulations, while also pushing for looser restrictions on selling AI chips to China. In December, Trump said Nvidia would be allowed to ship its H200 chips to China, provided the U.S. got a 25% cut of sales. In March, Huang told reporters that Nvidia had "received purchase orders, and we're in the process of restarting our manufacturing."

Huang was a last-minute addition to the roster of tech executives who joined Trump on a trip to China in May, after the president was briefed on intelligence concerns, according to the Times.

"In 35 years, I haven't seen a chip CEO with this much direct influence on export policy," said Patrick Moorhead, CEO at Moor Insights & Strategy, in an email.

Since last week's midterm elections that included sweeping victories across the country for Democrats, the mood in Washington has changed. Three House committees have requested closed-door briefings with the Nvidia CEO about his relationship with Megaspeed.

Democratic lawmakers, who are about to take power in the House and Senate, have said publicly that Congress will investigate and seek answers. Trump said Monday that he was "amazed" by the allegations and that Huang told him he "had no idea that she was a spy." The FBI is now looking into the issue, Trump said at a White House briefing.

It's been a long four years for Nvidia in China.

Before the initial export controls in 2022, China made up nearly a quarter of Nvidia's revenue. Nvidia responded to the Biden-era restrictions by redesigning some of its products to comply with the new regulations.

"It was a turbulent month or so as the company went upside down to re-engineer" the products, Huang told CNBC in a 2023 interview. Still, he was confident the company could continue selling chips to China. "I think we're going to be just fine in the ability to serve the customers there," he said.

After the Biden administration tightened export restrictions again, Nvidia redesigned another AI graphics processing unit for China, the H20. Following reports that Chinese model developer DeepSeek was using restricted Nvidia chips, the Trump administration limited sales of H20. Nvidia revealed it would incur $5.5 billion in costs related to canceled orders for the redesigned chip.

However, despite saying in its forecasts that "we're assuming 0% for China," the company maintained business in the region. In the last fiscal year, Nvidia reported that about 9% of its revenue came from China, including Hong Kong, although that includes revenue from segments outside of restricted data center chips, such as gaming, auto and industrial. Nvidia's China revenue was 8% in the most recent quarter.

A report in May from short seller Culper Research alleged Nvidia's China exposure was much higher. The firm said that more than 20% of Nvidia's fiscal 2026 compute revenue was driven by China, supported by "illegal GPU diversion and Southeast Asian intermediaries," according to the report.

A loophole allows Chinese companies to rent Nvidia GPUs in data centers, even when they're restricted from buying them outright. For example, a Chinese company could lease Nvidia computing power in Europe by renting server capacity from a non-Chinese neocloud or data center operator that's exempt from export controls.

Nvidia didn't respond to a request for comment for this story.

Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer based in Singapore, has been accused of diverting its own supply of Nvidia's restricted AI chips to Chinese companies. Megaspeed denies wrongdoing and said inspections found no evidence of violations.

In 2025, Nvidia changed the way it reported regional sales to categorize by customer headquarters, instead of by where the products were billed. Nvidia also stopped reporting Singapore sales during the same period.

"Whether Blackwell chips reached China is a Megaspeed question that predates this week," said Moorhead, pointing to Bloomberg reporting in December 2025 that found Megaspeed had imported 50,000 more Nvidia chips than Nvidia tallied in its data centers.

In 2025, a House Select Committee opened an investigation into whether China's DeepSeek operates on Nvidia's chips.

A separate investigation by the U.S. Commerce Department was centered on Megaspeed. That probe was abruptly transferred to the Justice Department, the Times reported, following an email from Nvidia's general counsel to the Commerce Department seeking information about the investigation.

The restrictions aren't just coming from the U.S. China has continued to limit the amount of Nvidia chips it will allow into the country, keeping Nvidia's China sales numbers down. The U.S. has licensed H200 sales to Alibaba, Tencent, ByteDance and other Chinese companies, but Beijing hasn't approved large-scale imports, Moorhead told CNBC. Nvidia said H200 shipments to China made up less than 1% of data center revenue for the quarter that ended in July.

Moorhead expects more export control legislation to pass as a result of the latest turmoil. Proposals could allow Congress oversight of chip export licenses or require chipmakers to install security mechanisms to track the location of chips to determine if they've found their way to China.

"Every China license now gets read through this story," Moorhead said. "Jensen has been Nvidia's lead voice on China, and that voice is weaker."

— CNBC's Kif Leswing and Seema Mody contributed reporting to this report.

Correction: Three House committees have requested closed-door briefings with the Nvidia CEO about his relationship with Megaspeed. An earlier version misstated the time element.
