Google DeepMind melds humans and AI agents to vet Signal's secure messaging techniques

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Post-quantum handshake and Triple Ratchet get a formal security check

Google DeepMind researchers have completed a formal verification of Signal's messaging security, marking what they claim is the first time AI has helped devise a proof that links protocol-level security guarantees to a widely used cryptographic implementation.

They did so using AlphaProof Code, a multi-agent framework for formal software specification and verification. AlphaProof Code relies on a human-guided agent that writes a Lean specification, assesses machine code semantics, and crafts an agent-authored proof that the Lean kernel checks against the specification.

In this instance, AlphaProof Code verified Signal’s post-quantum PQXDH handshake and Triple Ratchet, thereby validating the design of libsignal's forward secrecy, authentication, post-compromise security, and handshake secrecy.

In so doing, the Google/DeepMind team – Swarat Chaudhuri, Andrew Ferraiuolo, Anton Kovsharov, Tej Chajed, Sam Schlesinger, Karn Seth, Jonathan Katz, Charles Sutton, and Pushmeet Kohli – has taken a step toward a challenge the Beneficial AI Foundation issued in April to formally verify the Signal protocol and Signal app with Lean.

The AlphaProof Code process involves working with various models to turn Signal's security goals into a human-reviewed mathematical specification. Based on this spec, the researchers assigned Gemini Carbon agents to generate Lean code proving the correctness of the libsignal implementation. After the agents generated 6.5 million lines of Lean code, they undertook an auditing pass and flagged bits that required human review.

The result is theoretical in that it assumes perfect cryptography and doesn't guarantee the computational secrecy of bytes. But it's still a meaningful assertion of correctness.

"It's a system that is fundamentally built for human AI collaboration," said Swarat Chaudhuri, research scientist at Google DeepMind and a professor at the University of Texas at Austin, in an interview with The Register.

Traditionally, said Chaudhuri, you start proving theorems with well-defined problems formulated by a mathematician that get submitted to an AI agent for proof. In this case, the researchers had to figure out what to prove.

"In formal methods, we always had this belief that verification is the hard bit," he said. "There's thirty or forty years of work on how to scale up verification. Now we are able to do that. So now the question is what are we verifying? … That process is fundamentally human-in-the-loop, but we devised a process of trying to scale it up to the extent possible with AI."

As Chaudhuri and his colleagues note in their research paper, "AlphaProof Code: Verifying Signal’s Security at the Machine-Code Level," formal verification used to require heroic efforts. Formally verifying the seL4 microkernel took 20 person-years, while developing the verified CompCert C compiler took six years.

AlphaProof Code, they believe, can drastically reduce the cost of formal verification, though their Signal work still took four weeks for a team of five people and "quite a few tokens."

"The dream is definitely to scale this up," said Chaudhuri. "So you know there's been this rise in offensive AI capabilities, right? There are a lot of insecure systems around the world, and they're going to be exploited.

"So what we really need to do is to apply this kind of technology to code at large all over the world and figure out what is it that you want to guard against? How do you write those properties, how do we specify those properties? That's going to be super important, but we have to figure that out."

Pointing to a related DeepMind project, AlphaProof Nexus, which is focused on mathematics, Chaudhuri said the next step is to apply this technology during the software design process – not just to cryptographic protocols but code in general.

"We really believe that the next frontier is not going to be proof but specification in this kind of research," he said. "And so that's why we took on this domain where that was actually a hard problem. And I think that we have some really good insights now about how to go about this, although not the last word by any means." ®

The Grindr 1 Percent Is Getting Its Own Gated Community

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In a reddit AMA on Friday, in between jokes about gay bears and JD Vance’s potential username being CushionCasanova, Grindr CEO George Arison answered a question about the economy. He didn’t hold back.

“Inflation is terrible, and there’s a massive affordability crisis in this country that our government has ignored since at least 2020,” he wrote in the r/wallstreetbets subreddit, where, following the release of the company’s quarterly earnings report, he took questions from users.

It was a startling admission from the man whose company is about to release a subscription offering intended to price out most Grindr users. On November 17, Edge, a “premium tier” with some AI-powered features costing $120 a month, will be available to members worldwide, according to the report. Early access opened today.

The document suggests that Edge will help users find connections “with less effort” and will include a personalized recommendation feature, Insights, to “understand compatibility and the likelihood of a reply,” as well as Reconnect, which “brings past connections back into view.” The app will have an entirely new design, interface, and icon intended to create a vastly different experience for users willing to foot the high price tag.

But even if you can afford it, you may not get in. As with Raya, an invite-only app where some people have been on the wait list for as long as seven years, the company is planning to turn Edge into a digital gated community of sorts: Sign-ups will be limited to 1 percent of global monthly users.

The goal is to “deliberately [manage] the growth of the Edge community to preserve exclusivity” for “a select community” who can pay “meaningfully more,” the report noted. According to figures from the company, only 1.4 million of its current 16 million users pay to use Grindr.

Edge’s official rollout comes following pilot programs in Australia, New Zealand, Canada, and the US, and a year’s worth of speculation about how much it would cost and what exactly would separate it from other apps that provide comparable features at a lower price.

“These VIP tiers make me think of [it as a] digital version of expensive hustler bars,” one user said on Instagram. “The men who will pay hundreds for access are rarely the hot men you would log on for.”

Grindr is the world’s largest dating app for gay, bi, and trans people, but it has consistently struggled with user concerns of data privacy and user safety. And as it unveils its latest upsell, many members say the app’s fundamental infrastructure remains broken.

“Grindr is a top-tier example of enshitification and predatory monetization,” one person wrote in the reddit AMA from last week. “You need to stop with the intrusive ads and start getting in touch with reality,” posted another.

In September, the company agreed to a $35 million class action settlement over allegedly sharing users’ HIV status and testing information with third-party vendors; the details of the settlement included no admission of liability on Grindr’s behalf. That same month, Grindr acquired the telehealth company Freddie, which helps people get access to the HIV-prevention medication PrEP. The purchase builds on Arison’s investment into health care and turning the app into a social network. Currently, Grindr sells erectile dysfunction pills and GLP-1 weight-loss medication through its Woodwork program.

Critics will likely argue that pivoting to luxury dating in an economy where more and more people are struggling isn’t great optics, but Arison is betting that improved features and a “deliberately limited membership” will create opportunity for “substantially higher pricing over time,” the report noted.

Samsung’s paid Galaxy AI plan has a giant Gemini problem

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Good morning. Today I’m trying desperately not to buy Sony’s new nostalgia-bait headphones, reeling from how emotionally invested I was in Spider-Man: Brand New Day (yes, I know I’m late), trying to figure out what a “private, agentic notebook” is, and slowly coming around to the idea of the New York Times owning Letterboxd.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

The relationship between Samsung and Google has, over the years, waxed and waned depending on their respective interdependence. Samsung is Android’s physical emissary, powering a plurality of all its handsets globally. But Google, and in particular its services, is the glue that holds Samsung’s smartphone experience together.

The back and forth is over a decade old; back in early 2014, Samsung shipped a refreshed magazine-style skin with its Galaxy tablets that eschewed many of the design tenets Google had been pushing its partners to adhere to. Not only was Samsung willfully defying its most important partner, but it made a point of barely mentioning Android during its press conferences.

Around the same time, it transitioned its smartwatches from Android Wear to Tizen and began shipping duplicative apps on its devices (many of which persist to this day). The goal was to distance itself from Google as much as possible.

While we don’t know exactly what happened behind closed doors, we saw the outcome quite clearly: Magazine UX lasted one product cycle, and the two companies signed a 10-year cross-license patent deal, which may have helped Samsung prevent any future lawsuits with Apple. Google then sold Motorola to Lenovo, clearing the way for Samsung to continue building its burgeoning hardware business on the back of Google’s mobile ecosystem.

Since then, the two companies have grown even closer. In 2021, they co-announced a new Wear OS along with an overarching wearables partnership that has since grown to include Android XR as well. And Google is still the default search engine for Samsung devices, despite reported inroads made by Microsoft in 2023 to convince the Korean giant to switch to a ChatGPT-powered version of Bing Search. (Samsung’s ongoing relationship with Microsoft, which bundles its Office suite on most Galaxy devices, could be seen as a point of leverage for negotiations with Google, too.)

This brings us to today, and to AI. Samsung was among the first companies to adopt Google’s full suite of Gemini Intelligence products, and its phones often get new AI features before anyone else. But like a decade ago, when Samsung shipped Bixby to kind-of-sort-of compete with Google Assistant, it still wants to offer its own services alongside Google’s.

So a rumor that Samsung may launch a paid Galaxy AI Premium subscription next year for $5/month piqued my interest for the same reason Magazine UX did 12 years ago. Galaxy AI features today are a blend of first-party and third-party capabilities, but none of them are especially useful, particularly when compared to Google’s own Android equivalents powered by Gemini Intelligence. Now Bar and Now Brief, two heavily promoted features in Samsung’s marketing, are wildly disappointing.

At I/O this past May, Google announced Spark, an agent that goes out and performs actions on your behalf. And while it’s since been somewhat eclipsed by more capable mobile options like Meta’s Muse, it’s obvious that Google is working on a follow-up that will perform functions both on the phone and inside apps.

That’s exactly what Samsung is rumored to be working on with Bixby Action, a spiritual successor to its namesake that, when it debuted, prioritized on-device tasks like turning on Wi-Fi over web-based actions Google Assistant did better.

According to leaks from SamMobile, Galaxy AI Premium will offer agentic upgrades for phone users to “create commands triggers and system routines for daily tasks” or “automate routine daily tasks using Galaxy AI.” Gen Video will reportedly “convert static photos into dynamic video clips,” while a new Daily Briefing likely does what it says on the tin.

Will Samsung users see enough value in this hodgepodge of features paywalled only because they use a surfeit of compute? And what models will power them on the backend?

Right now, Google is in the enviable position of being able to offer its AI at three distinct layers: the model itself, which is invisible to the user; the OS level, which can promulgate Gemini across every Android phone that runs Google services; and the hardware level, where Pixel lets it specialize and optimize.

Samsung, not particularly known for its software prowess, will likely need to step gingerly around Google’s AI shadow while also justifying the cost to shore up its much-needed services revenue. Based on this leaked roadmap, though, I’m not confident it will work out.

Instead, Samsung may want to acquire one of the many up-and-coming AI agent startups whose valuations are rising more quickly than their user bases. While Muse and OpenAI’s Dots have taken up most of the recent conversation, I’ve been using Hark, Town, and Instinct pretty regularly for a month or so and could see them being brought in-house by Samsung and offered either as standalone products or customized for Galaxy devices. As Google is doing with its own AI plans, Samsung could provide Galaxy users a year of free service, after which they would be expected to pay.

Samsung doesn’t have a great history of building software, but many of its acquisitions, from SmartThings to Viv Labs (which became “next-gen” Bixby) to LoopPay (which became Samsung Pay) to Whisk (which became Samsung Food), have proven successful. Given the current urgent state of mobile AI, I’d forgo the hodgepodge and bring in some outside expertise.

Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy is the only thing tech media can talk about right now, likely because it has implications far beyond just Nvidia’s stock price. The Nvidia CEO, who is one of the world’s richest and most powerful tech executives, is believed to have been targeted by Chinese intelligence to potentially influence American trade policy around AI.

Alex Kantrowitz’s Big Technology piece is a useful starting point for why this matters at a global scale:

Beyond the affair, the real issue is that Huang’s love interest, ex-Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang, is someone the United States government believes passed information to the Chinese government while building data centers with Nvidia’s help in Asia. There’s simply no getting away from this detail. This isn’t just an affair, it’s a corporate and national security issue.

Whether Huang can survive the impending investigation by Nvidia’s board remains to be seen, but the CEO has been a stalwart in the tech space for 33 years, and there is no clear successor to his position should he be forced to resign.

Damien reviewed DroidDeck, which I wrote about last week as part of a broader look at Android’s gaming renaissance.

Over the last few days, things have become even more preposterous: the Ocarina of Time remake, which was released on Thursday, has already been recompiled to run natively on Android (though it runs better on PC), largely with the help of frontier AI models.

All of these AI-powered shenanigans come after Anthropic said it would stop making it possible to recreate copies of games, claiming that “recreating someone else’s specific game using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.” Good thing there are no other models capable of doing the same thing.

I think when you zoom out and look at production, AI really has the potential to be the biggest creative unlock in modern history. The last time we saw anything close to this was really the, you know, advent of CGI and digital composites in the early ’90s. That technology gave birth to amazing stories like Jurassic Park and Terminator 2, and it’s hard to imagine that AI is not going to have a more significant impact than that. And I personally believe that’s going to be an incredible unlock on creativity.

—David Ellison, Skydance CEO, when asked about how AI could impact the movie-making process

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Thursday.

Bybit Says $17.3B Proof of Reserves Covers 50 Assets, but the Audit Has Limits

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Bybit says the latest snapshot of its proof-of-reserves system covered $17.3 billion in customer assets across 50 cryptocurrencies. The exchange's November 3 announcement describes its 41st report, with figures independently checked by security firm Hacken against an October 27 snapshot.

Bybit says the latest snapshot of its proof-of-reserves system covered $17.3 billion in customer assets across 50 cryptocurrencies. The exchange's November 3 announcement describes its 41st report, with figures independently checked by security firm Hacken against an October 27 snapshot. The exercise offers a view of assets and customer balances at a specific moment, but it should not be mistaken for an all-purpose guarantee that an exchange could meet every liability under every scenario.

Bybit says its reserve ratios exceeded 100% across all 50 covered assets, including 112% for USDT, 405% for USDC, 102% for Bitcoin and 101% for Ether. Those percentages compare identified reserves with customer balances under the stated methodology. An unusually high ratio for one token does not mean the business has the same excess in every asset or that reserves could be freely moved between customer pools. Readers should also distinguish a token-by-token reserve ratio from consolidated corporate solvency, which incorporates obligations that may not appear in a proof-of-reserves snapshot.

The release identifies the audit point as October 27 at 06:00 UTC. Reserve balances, withdrawals, borrowing, market prices and off-exchange exposures may have changed after that time. The value of recurring disclosures lies partly in their comparability: users can look for persistent gaps or unexpected movements rather than treating a single publication as permanent reassurance. Bybit says the checks covered the 50 listed crypto assets, but the exact perimeter, included customer categories and handling of institutional arrangements are questions worth reviewing in the detailed report.

The exchange says Hacken carried out the verification. Third-party involvement is useful, particularly when the reviewer explains its methods and limitations, but the word 'audit' can be interpreted too broadly. A Merkle-tree exercise may allow customers to verify that a balance was included while proving possession of identified onchain assets. It does not, by itself, necessarily establish that there are no undisclosed debts, encumbrances, affiliated-party claims or operational weaknesses. The scope of work therefore deserves as much attention as the name of the verifier.

Asset backing is a fundamental question for custodial exchanges because users depend on the firm to return assets they cannot directly control. Yet security controls, market surveillance, legal ownership of customer property, segregation practices and withdrawal resilience also matter. A fully funded snapshot is only one component of a broader risk picture. The report can be especially informative when combined with clear explanations of liabilities, independent financial statements where available and timely incident reporting. It should not substitute for personal decisions about custodial concentration.

Bybit users can inspect the methodology and verify balances if the relevant tool supports that check. External analysts should compare successive reports and look for consistency between claimed assets and observable onchain holdings. Markets benefit from more systematic disclosures, but transparent limitations are an important part of credible transparency. The milestone is the reporting process, not a declaration that counterparty risk has disappeared.

Primary source: Bybit proof-of-reserves announcement. For additional context, see NewsBTC’s analysis of earnings and stablecoin activity at another major exchange.

Feature: These Are Xbox's Best 25 Games From 2001-2026 According To Metacritic

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

As we continue our celebrations for Xbox's 25th anniversary this Sunday (November 15th), we thought it might be fun to look back at the past 25 years on Metacritic, and specifically the best-rated Xbox games from each year.

The website has a significant history of review roundups dating all the way back to 2001, and looking at the list, the early years in particular are jam-packed with historic Xbox games! Halo is in there multiple times, as is Gears of War and Grand Theft Auto, and then we start to see an indie explosion with the likes of Inside and Hades in later years.

So, here are Xbox's best 25 games from 2001-2026 according to Metacritic from each individual year. Just keep in mind that we've eliminated a couple of expansions, as we're only counting full game releases.

Of course, when it comes to 2026 specifically, there's every chance GTA 6 will end up taking the top spot within a couple of weeks' time, so just keep that in mind - Hades 2 has done amazingly well, but will most likely be surpassed.

And by the way, this list doesn't tell the full story - here's what it would look like if you took the individual years away:

Pretty different, right? It's no surprise that there were multiple years that fared better than others in terms of highly-rated releases, so the likes of The Orange Box, Red Dead Redemption 2, Portal 2, Halo 3 and others still make the lineup.

Anyway, Metacritic doesn't tell the full story by any means here, but it's a useful resource to look back and see how well the critics positioned these games at the time, and whether we reckon they're still justifying their high scores.

So, what do you reckon to these? We'd love to hear your thoughts down in the comments section below.

Guide The Best Original Xbox Games Of All Time

Including exclusives & backwards compatible titles

Fraser is the News Editor at Pure Xbox, where he spends his time reporting on the biggest stories in the world of Xbox and beyond.

There is undoubtedly a time when review scores became more inline with the scores we see today. In other words, there is no way that Halo: Combat Evolved would score 97 under todays review score regime. Don't misunderstand me, Halo: CE is still a great game, but if reviewed today it would be marked down for its samey locations, limited enemy types etc. etc. (and I just picked that game, rather than picking on that game).

I'd say we don't judge games based on their graphical prowess because otherwise all these retro style games would be marked down severely, which obviously does not happen.

Gains in consoles are so incremental these days that I think a game that released from 2015 onwards is probably judged by much the same standards now as it was back then.

What would be interesting, would be for someone to take up reviewing games, and to review older games but by todays standards. So someone that had never played the first Gears of War, plays it through, and gives it a review score based not on the wow, but on its technical achievements, level design, enemy variety, and what have you. I imagine that we would see a very different set of scores if games were reviewed now from what they were given when they originally released, but it would be interesting to see just how these older games would fair...

@Fiendish-Beaver I agree it is harder to get to that 95ish score than it was. I think there's a few different things going on there.

Firstly as @Kaloudz said there was a big graphical jump between generations. But there was also much larger GAMEPLAY jumps back then too. Games and their mechanics were often breaking new paradigms whether that was Mario or Zelda in 3D for the first time or Gears and Halo pushing third and third person console shooters into the mainstream on console. It felt exciting. Now most games feel much more iterative, especially AAA games.

There were also typically fewer reviews for each game, and those reviews were generally from a smaller cross-section of gamers in similar demographics who were more likely to have similar tastes, that led to homogenisation of some scores. Now a game might be marked down by a new demographic, which is fair enough, aggregate scores don't tell the whole story, we all like different things.

Linked to that I know some people believe that Nintendo games get unfairly scored higher than they might for some reason, but I don't see it that way. I think there is often a universal joy to Nintendo games that resonates with far more players. More simply put: they haven't forgotten how to make their games joyful and fun on an almost primal level. That won't be for everyone - nothing is - but it is for a wider cross section of people, and reviewers, hence they are more likely to score higher as an aggregate.

Look at Ocarina of Time remake. Not only a hit with critics currently scoring 96 on Metacritic from 94 reviews but also currently a 9.6 from 10.5k user reviews, it's rare to see both critics and users in such harmony. Having swallowed it whole and finished it already, I would agree it's the easiest 10 in years imho. Bring on GTA6.

As for the list as you'd expect with a best of list it's wall to wall bangers. I'm surprised there's more games than I thought on that list I haven't played yet, but I really struggle to start 100+ hour games like Persona 5 or BG3. One day!

AlphaProof Code

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

How Google DeepMind researchers built a new agentic framework that formally verified the code of Signalâs cryptographic protocol â long considered to be a grand security challenge

Today, weâre sharing a milestone in software security long considered to be a grand challenge: Google DeepMind researchers guided AI agents to mathematically prove key security properties of the actual code underlying the Signal protocol, the technology that protects billions of private conversations in apps like Signal and Google Messages.

This breakthrough discovery was made using AlphaProof Code, a novel agentic framework for formal verification at unprecedented depth that combines human expertise with AI agents. Formal verificationâmathematically proving code runs correctly without flawsâis the highest standard of security assurance; however, it is difficult and often labor-intensive.

Our work marks the first time AI has been used to formally verify a widely deployed cryptographic protocol at this depth. It's also the first time the implementation of a widely deployed protocol like this has been formally verified by any method, with or without AI.

AlphaProof Code is a multi-LLM agentic framework that splits the work of formal verification the way a research team would: human experts work with AI to decide what to prove, and a group of AI agents does it.

First, our researchers collaborated with a mix of models to translate Signalâs security goals into formal mathematical specifications, with human experts reviewing every one. From there, agents powered by Gemini Carbon did the proofs, generating over six million lines of Lean, a computer language that mathematically proves the accuracy of code.

Next, the agents audited the proof and highlighted areas that needed a second look by a human â just one percent of the codebase, a massive time savings. Because Lean automatically checks the rest, our experts could focus their review where human judgment matters most.

Cryptographic protocols underpin nearly all digital communication, from online banking to web browsing, and a single flaw can expose millions of people at once. That's why experts have spent decades formally analyzing them.

But most protocol verification stops at the design level. Our agents went further, proving that the compiled code fulfills the key security properties â such as keeping messages protected before and after a device is temporarily compromised â down to the exact code running on devices. Because real-world vulnerabilities often hide in the gap between design and implementation, bridging this gap makes these proofs vastly more complex.

Even at this complexity, our verification process took just four weeks â a task that historically takes expert teams years â proving that Signal's core cryptographic code delivers the security protections our experts specified.

To confirm that AlphaProof Code can tell correct code from buggy code, we reintroduced two real, previously fixed bugs into an isolated version of Signal's code. In both cases, the system produced a machine-checked proof that the buggy version was insecure.

In AlphaProof Code, AI agents help users write a specification, then autonomously prove that the implementation meets it. Every proof is checked by Lean to verify the agents' work.

While prior formal verification focused on smaller portions of the code, Google DeepMind's approach enables significant expansion,â said Ehren Kret, Chief Technology Officer of Signal. âUsing AI agents to translate live code into valid Lean theorems is a major breakthrough. As AI-driven security threats grow, rigorously verifying the Signal protocol implementation gives us even greater confidence in our code's privacy guarantees.

While prior formal verification work has necessarily focused on smaller scopes, this new approach enables a significant expansion. Traditionally, verifying that production code strictly matches its cryptographic design required extensive manual effort, making the use of AI agents to automatically translate live code into valid Lean theorems a great breakthrough. As AI-driven security threats grow, having the core Signal protocol rigorously verified with automated theorem proving gives us even greater confidence that our code delivers on its privacy guarantees.

Achieving this long-standing challenge demonstrates how AI can bring elite security assurance to businesses, governments, and critical infrastructure, helping defend against agent-driven cyber threats.

Verifying Signal's core cryptography lays the groundwork to secure other vital systems, from medical device firmware to power grid software.

Scaling these techniques requires continued research. Paired with automated defense agents like CodeMender, formal verification can help counter the speed and scale of modern cyber threats to create software with mathematical proof that its protections work.

We are open-sourcing our formal verification artifacts for community use. Read the technical paper below.

This research was developed by Swarat Chaudhuri, Andrew Ferraiuolo, Anton Kovsharov, Tej Chajed, Sam Schlesinger, Karn Seth, Jonathan Katz, Charles Sutton, and Pushmeet Kohli.

We thank Eric Wieser for help with open-sourcing our code, Owen Jones for program management support, and MartÃ­n Abadi, Christopher Henson, Katerina Hristova, Henryk Michalewski, Jonathan Protzenko and George Tsoukalas for helpful discussions. We thank Signal's Rolfe Schmidt, Michael Rosenberg, and Ehren Kret for their feedback on our methodology. We also thank Tulsee Doshi, Four Flynn, Kate Olszewska, Alicia Huang, Andy Liu, Benoit Schillings, Raluca Ada Popa, Melvin Johnson, Koray Kavukcuoglu, Demis Hassabis, and Sundar Pichai for their support of the project.

Atletico Madrid eye January move for Arsenal midfielder Martin Zubimendi

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Atletico Madrid are looking to make an ambitious January swoop for Martin Zubimendi after the Arsenal midfielder failed to convince Mikel Arteta to give him a regular starting spot.

The Spain international has started all seven of his Premier League appearances this season, coming off the bench. This has reportedly caught the eye of Diego Simeone, who wants to bolster his midfield in the winter window.

Atletico have marked Zubimendi as a possible target, according to Spanish radio station RNE via SPORT. Sporting director Mateu Alemany feels that there is still more to do with Simeone’s squad despite big investments in new players this summer.

If Zubimendi’s situation fails to improve at Arsenal, he could become one of the most wanted signings of January.

Zubimendi played a major role in Arsenal’s Premier League title-winning campaign last season. He made 34 league starts, scoring five goals and providing one assist.

He played a key role in the club’s run to the Champions League final. Since then Arsenal’s midfield has changed.

Injuries have hampered his campaign, but Myles Lewis-Skelly has returned to the midfield, and Bruno Guimaraes joined in the summer.

The 27-year-old has also expressed frustration at the lack of playing opportunities available to him. He was signed for £60 million from Real Sociedad to Arsenal just 18 months ago and has three-and-a-half years left on his contract.

Any move to Atletico would be a battle. Real Madrid are also reportedly fans of the midfielder, and he has also been linked with a move to Chelsea in recent months.

Atletico are keen to bring Zubimendi in alongside Pablo Barrios in central midfield.

It would be a dream combination for Simeone, and the Spanish club are willing to fund any move.

Another option could see him involved in a deal for Julian Alvarez. There are currently no talks about a potential swap deal.

Arteta has previously played down concerns about Zubimendi’s importance to Arsenal. Earlier this month, he highlighted the midfielder’s commitment and suggested injuries had affected his opportunities.

The Arsenal manager also made clear that Zubimendi would have chances to play, but would need to take them when they arrived.

That leaves the midfielder with an opportunity to work his way back into Arteta’s plans. However, Atletico’s reported interest could add another dimension to his situation as January approaches.

Skydance Games lay off an undisclosed number of staff in the wake of Paramount-Warner Bros. merger, in latest case of consolidation killing livelihoods

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A little over a month following the huge, vile, culture-defining (negative) merger between Paramount and Warner Bros. into Skydance, the corporation has confirmed that games division Skydance Games will be facing a round of layoffs, according to a report from Variety.

Skydance Games was born from combining Paramount and Warner Bros' own individual divisions, bringing developers like NetherRealm, Rocksteady, WB Games Montreal/Boston, TT Games and Avalanche under the same umbrella as Marvel 1943: Rise of Hydra developers and Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles: The Last Ronin publishers Paramount Games Studio. All this was was done, of course, to maximise the amount of IP that the corporation can access to base games on, such as Avatar: The Last Airbender, Game of Thrones, Star Trek, the DC Universe, Harry Potter, and more. Skydance Games came into existence on October 6th; it barely lasted a month as it was.

In a statement given to Variety, Skydance confirmed that "As part of our ongoing efforts to position Skydance Games for long-term growth and success, we are implementing reorganization and operational shifts across a few areas of our business. We have stepped back and taken some time to consider where we are investing our efforts and have made the difficult decision to scale down some of our projects. As a result, there are roles impacted. This is not a reflection of the talent, dedication, or creativity of those affected and we are grateful for their contributions. We are committed to approaching the process with transparency, care and respect for those who are leaving the company." Skydance did not disclose the number of staff affected.

Skydance joins the ever-growing list of corporations that have mandated layoffs in the recent past, such as Microsoft, Ubisoft, and EA. Workers will bleed until there is no one left to cut. Solidarity to all affected.

HIVE reports $79 million quarterly net loss as bitcoin mining revenue falls and AI business grows

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

HIVE booked a $31.2 million impairment charge against its bitcoin mining equipment during the quarter and reported $185 million in contracted GPU cloud annualized run-rate revenue.

Bitcoin mining firm HIVE Digital Technologies HIVE Digital Technologies Ltd. (HIVE) reported a $78.7 million net loss in its fiscal second quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with a $15.8 million loss a year earlier.

The company generated $84.9 million in revenue, down 2.7% year over year, according to its latest earnings release on Monday.

HIVE received 1,070 BTC in mining rewards during the quarter, a 49% increase from 718 BTC in the same period last year. Mining revenue was $76 million, down from $82.1 million a year ago. Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) averaged $71,003 during the quarter, against $114,382 in the year-earlier period, which HIVE cited as the main reason for the decline in its 10-Q filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

HIVE also booked a $31.2 million impairment after assessing the recoverable value of its mining equipment. Depreciation amounted to another $54.9 million, and adjusted EBITDA was $24.8 million.

Meanwhile, the company's BUZZ High Performance Computing business generated $8.8 million in revenue, up 70.7% from $5.2 million last year. HIVE deployed an Nvidia B200 GPU cluster at its Manitoba site in Canada during the period, with GPU marketplace demand contributing to the increase.

In June, The Block reported that the company had made $297.8 million in revenue in fiscal 2026. High-performance computing accounted for a record $19.5 million of that amount.

The company said it has approximately $40 million in live GPU cloud annualized run-rate revenue. Its contracted figure is now approximately $185 million, including capacity that has yet to enter service. HIVE previously signed a three-year agreement with Bell AI Fabric supporting Cohere in June for 2,304 Nvidia Grace Blackwell GPUs. In August, HIVE also signed a $350 million deal to supply 2,016 NVIDIA Blackwell Ultra GPUs to an unnamed enterprise customer over five years.

CEO Aydin Kilic said Monday that the company had secured $129 million in financing for the Bell and Cohere GPU purchase, and he expects those GPUs to ship before the end of 2026. HIVE's near-term goal is $200 million in GPU cloud ARR in the fourth quarter of 2026, followed by a longer-term target of $700 million in total HPC ARR by the end of 2028.

The company ended September with $139 million in cash and cash equivalents and $22.6 million in digital currencies, Kilic said. HIVE currently operates approximately 440 MW of power capacity and expects to energize another 100 MW at Yguazú in Paraguay in the fourth quarter.

Binance Shuts Down Transfers Involving MEXC and Its Affiliates

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Just a few days after the U.S. Treasury took action to cut global crypto exchange MEXC off from the U.S. financial system, Binance has also announced strict regulatory actions against the exchange.

On Tuesday, November 10, Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange by volume, announced its immediate decision to halt all transfers involving MEXC and all its affiliated companies.

Binance released an official announcement earlier today, warning users that it has stopped all trading services related to MEXC following new concerns about its legitimacy.

While the restrictions were announced to take effect immediately, Binance explained that its move follows the recent decision by the U.S. Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) to designate MEXC as a Primary Money Laundering Concern.

As such, Binance has prohibited its users from directly or indirectly sending funds to, receiving funds from, or conducting transactions through Binance involving MEXC and its related entities.

The MEXC-affiliated entities mentioned by Binance include MEXC Global Limited, MEXC Estonia OÜ, MEXC Singapore Pte Ltd, MEXC Global Foundation Ltd, GIDA Technology AG, and several other companies and affiliates operating under the MEXC name.

Although MEXC is among the top 20 crypto exchanges in the world, FinCEN issued an order against MEXC on November 6, cutting the exchange off from the U.S. financial system amid recent fraud allegations.

Notably, the U.S. Treasury accused the exchange of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to sanctions evasion and cybercrime and of defrauding people of their funds.

While Binance remains the first crypto exchange to take action in accordance with FinCEN’s rule, it appears that many other exchanges are lining up to take similar action.

AWS commits $100M in AI and cloud credits to expand health services

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The five-year AWS Health Impact Initiative backs projects for underserved communities, women’s and children’s health, and nutrition.

Amazon Web Services (AWS) is putting $100M worth of AI and cloud technology into health projects for underserved communities around the world. The AWS Health Impact Initiative will run for five years.

Rather than cash, organisations receive credits to use AWS’s AI tools, AI agents and cloud computing. Health charities, government bodies, researchers and companies can all apply, from early prototypes to full services.

The aim is to stop the gap in health care from growing as AI spreads. According to the World Economic Forum, generative AI use will grow faster in health care than in any other sector. If organisations serving poorer communities fall behind, AWS warns, health inequalities will widen.

“Agents and AI are revolutionizing health services, but these transformative technologies too often don’t reach the communities that need them most unless organizations know how to access and deploy them,” said Dr. Rowland Illing, chief medical officer and director, Healthcare and Life Sciences at AWS.

Too often, however, these tools do not reach the communities that need them most, Illing added. The initiative is meant to help the organisations that serve them build with AI.

Although the programme funds health projects of all kinds, it focuses on three areas. The first is wider access to care, from community health workers to hospitals, especially in rural areas and in low- and middle-income countries.

The second is research into women’s and children’s health. Only 5% of health research funding goes to women’s health, and up to half of the medicines given to children have not been clinically tested on them, AWS said. The third is access to healthy food, as a quarter of children worldwide have stunted growth because of poor nutrition.

Among current users, Eve’s Ark saves women’s health research data that might otherwise be lost when labs close or grants end, and has stored a petabyte of it so far.

In India, the Akshaya Patra Foundation uses AWS to run kitchens that serve school meals to more than 2.35 million children each school day.

Successful applicants get credits based on their estimated costs, or up to $10,000 to get started. The credits last 12 months and can be extended to 24.

The programme follows AWS’s Health Equity Initiative, launched in 2021, which gave $60m in credits to more than 400 organisations. Since then, AWS says it has supported more than 600 health groups in over 80 countries.

The launch also adds to Amazon’s wider push into health care. Its Health AI assistant recently opened to all US customers, and it has launched an AI Bio platform for early drug discovery.

‘I’m lucky to have grown up around artists’: Cora Sheibani on her path to becoming a jeweller

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Cora Sheibani wearing jewellery from her own collections: a bronze bracelet (from Pottering Around collection), Renaissance earrings in platinum and a sapphire facet ring in 18K red gold (both from Facets & Forms Collection)

Art of Luxury magazine, published twice per year by The Art Newspaper, explores how grande marque fashion, jewellery, travel and lifestyle interact with artists, the art market and the museums and heritage sector.

The jeweller Cora Sheibani lives in London and works from a small studio in Mayfair. She was raised in the art world—her father was the celebrated Swiss dealer Bruno Bischofberger, who collaborated with artists including Jean-Michel Basquiat, Andy Warhol and Francesco Clemente. As a dyslexic, Cora did not embrace reading as a child, but now she explains why it is her window on the world.

For me, luxury is about having time. As you get older, and as the world gets faster, there’s never enough of it. In an ideal world I would travel more, both for work and for pleasure; and visit exhibitions, galleries and museums. But I also have a son who needs a lot of my attention—he is very autistic, with severe ADHD—and so I travel through books, and reading has become a way to experience culture, art and creativity.

I’m lucky to have grown up around artists and people who didn’t follow the conventional path. Francesco Clemente is a close family friend, and his daughters are more like my cousins. We’re close to the Schnabel family; Jean Tinguely was my sister’s godfather. So even though I barely read as a child—it was just too hard—I made myself study liberal arts before I became a jeweller. Only after university I started reading German literature. It’s my mother language, after all.

I’ve always loved jewellery. When I was 15, I worked in my parents’ gallery over the summer, and spent half my earnings on two rings, both bronze, one Greek, one Roman. Now I work primarily with gold: at first it was because yellow is my favourite colour, but you also learn that gold is the friendliest metal to handle.

It was hard to be taken seriously as a jeweller at the beginning. People thought it was a hobby, and I really had to be patient and persevere—that’s something I can do, that dyslexia taught me. Now I’m incredibly proud of my work and how successful the business has become.

Jewellery books and especially exhibition catalogues are a real inspiration. An essay by Amin Jaffer in the catalogue for Joyaux Dynastiques, a show at the Hôtel de la Marine in Paris last year of royal jewels, explained the meaning of a particular kind of nine-gem Indian jewel. It really helped me with a commission I’d just received from a client for a piece using nine different coloured stones.

I buy some early editions from Shapero Rare Books in Mayfair—I pass it every time I go from the gym to the studio and can’t resist taking a look. I just finished reading Crime and Punishment by Fyodor Dostoyevsky in its first English translation by Frederick Whishaw. It’s very rare, a real collector’s item. It’s generally thought that translations have improved, but there’s something special about reading a contemporaneous one. It’s a rich experience that takes you back in time.

Once a month, I get together with my book club. There’s only three of us, and we don’t all have to read the same book. Instead, we talk about what we are each reading. We meet, usually on a Friday morning, at Daria Buchman’s house, and have a breakfast of scrambled eggs. Daria, with her husband Dimitry, sponsors the International Booker Prize, so she is really on top of every new novel. She hadn’t read Beloved by Toni Morrison, though. I’d read three works that talked about slavery from different regional angles—Beloved, Crooked Plow by Itamar Vieira Junior and Ben Okri’s The Last Gift of the Master Artists—and recommended to my group they did the same. You begin to understand a bit better how the lives of people from one continent evolved in such different (and at times similar) ways. On the other hand, Daria made me read the wonderful Wuthering Heights. I wouldn’t have done it if she hadn’t insisted.

Frédéric Boucheron created the first necklace without a clasp so that women could put it on unaided

London-based Dutch artist uses reassembled broken china fragments to create five unique vessels

Douriean Fletcher, who made the jewellery for Black Panther, speaks at the National Museum of Women in the Arts

Called Serenissima, the jewels use finely wrought metals to capture the art of the lace makers found on the island of Burano

arts at CERN translate the mechanics of the universe into creative installations

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

art and physics meet through works that translate invisible particles, experimental data, and abstract dimensions into sensory experiences.

annalise kamegawa November 10, 2026 7 min

When asked, ‘What does CERN do?’ most people would probably scratch their heads and respond, ‘Physics.’ As the European Laboratory for Particle Physics in Geneva, it most certainly does physics, but another thing it does—alongside maintaining a Large Hadron Collider and developing inventions like the World Wide Web—is invite artists from around the world to produce work in tandem with the sciences. Through its artist residencies, commissions, and programs, it has invited the likes of Tomás Saraceno, Ryoji Ikeda, and other renowned artists to create works that, much like the laboratory’s research, demystify the world around us.

There’s a feeling that the principles of physics, especially the discoveries made during the 20th century, have unlocked the mysteries of the universe. Films like Oppenheimer (2023) and Interstellar (2014) attempt to show the power of these principles through atomic bombs, time dilation, and wormholes. Yet, when sitting in front of an equation like e=mc², how many of us know what to make of it? When peering into the wires, buttons, and lights of a laboratory, what signals make it through to the brain? This is where the power of art comes into play at CERN—not only can it help transform these ideas into comprehensible forms, but it can also, at times, suggest new applications for these scientific tools.

Take, for example, Julius von Bismarck’s 2023 sculpture, Round About Four Dimensions. The artist, who apprenticed at the Berlin Institute for Spatial Experiments, created a ‘tesseract,’ or essentially a ‘shadow’ of a four-dimensional cube. When describing the project—a webbed, cube-like structure made of aluminum, stainless steel, brass, polymers, and electronics—Arts at CERN says of von Bismarck’s work, ‘This three-dimensional revolving motion is a projection of a four-dimensional rotation, a phenomenon we cannot fully grasp. This previously intangible concept is brought to life by the kinetic tesseract sculpture, which wraps endlessly around itself.’

With 32 expanding and contracting lengths, or edges, the sculpture looks as though it swallows the form at its center or turns inside out. Using a series of software-programmed controls, the sculpture brings viewers closer to understanding the mechanics of four-dimensional objects.

CERN is largely known for being home to the Large Hadron Collider, a machine that launches protons at nearly the speed of light. With this machine, researchers can pursue different experiments, like ALICE, which studies, ‘the physics of strongly interacting matter at extreme energy densities, where a phase of matter called quark-gluon plasma forms.’ For their 2018 project HALO, the artistic group Semiconductor created a work that sought to illustrate the experiments behind ATLAS, a ‘general-purpose detector,’ or a series of cameras and documentation equipment inside the Large Hadron Collider that tracks the results of experiments conducted within it.

Commissioned by Audemars Piguet in collaboration with CERN, HALO was a cylindrical installation that visualized the flow of data from ATLAS in an inhabitable installation that recalled the actual tube-like form of the machine. During their 2015 residency at CERN, the group became familiar with how the machines worked. In the 2018 work, they took the mass of information coming from ATLAS and slowed it down into sounds and flashes of light that are perceivable to the human eye.

Art Basel 2018 – Semiconductor’s artwork titled “HALO” | Audemars Piguet

Alongside its commissions and exhibitions, CERN also organizes residencies that bring artists into contact with its advanced machinery, research facilities, and scientists. Most recently, in collaboration with the Nobel Prize Museum, it awarded Collide Stockholm, its international residency award, to Lithuanian-born artist Emilija Škarnulytė. CERN describes her practice as ‘rooted in the exploration of infrastructures that mediate between the visible and the invisible, the human and the post-human, the present and deep time.’ Through meditative moving images and immersive audiovisual installations, Škarnulytė explores the hidden systems and processes that shape the natural world, often shifting the viewer’s perspective beyond the limits of human perception.

Ultimately, what these collaborations at CERN reveal is that art and science are not such distant disciplines. Both are driven by a desire to understand what lies beyond the limits of human perception, whether that means exploring the smallest particles of matter, imagining dimensions we cannot physically experience, or uncovering the hidden infrastructures of the natural world. By translating complex scientific processes into sensory, spatial, and immersive encounters, artists offer us new ways of relating to the universe. More than simply making science accessible, these collaborations challenge our understanding of humanity’s place within it, suggesting that seeing the world differently may be the first step towards understanding it.

This article is part of designboom’s POSTHUMAN UTOPIAS chapter, exploring what happens when humans are no longer the measure of all things. Bitcoin trading picked up, but sellers took the initiative

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin faces a key inflation test near $75,000 as Glassnode data shows sellers dominating spot trading and ETF outflows deepening.

Bitcoin's latest weekly trading data showed sellers retaining the initiative even as turnover rose, leaving its recovery near $75,000 without a clear sign that aggressive buyers were taking control.

The Nov. 9 report from blockchain analytics firm Glassnode sets a cautious starting point ahead of today's US inflation release.

Reuters reported closing gains of about 2.4% for the S&P 500 and 2.7% for the Nasdaq Composite on Nov. 9, with cheaper oil helping relieve inflation worries. Bitcoin was trading near $75,000 on Nov. 9, according to Glassnode.

Glassnode's latest Market Pulse put spot cumulative volume delta (CVD) at negative $92 million, compared with positive $7 million seven days earlier. Its spot volume indicator rose 13.3% to $6.4 billion from $5.6 billion.

CVD tracks the balance between aggressive buying and selling within the activity Glassnode measures. Negative CVD means aggressive selling outweighed aggressive buying in that measured activity.

The report's weekly indicators describe the demand conditions Bitcoin brought into the equity rally.

Glassnode's November 9 weekly snapshot shows spot activity rising while aggressive order flow turned negative, compared with seven days earlier.

Higher trading volume can accompany seller aggression. It does not by itself confirm renewed buying pressure.

Weekly report comparison, not Monday US-session data. Spot volume is an indicator, not a stated calendar-week total. Negative CVD concerns aggressive order flow within measured activity; every trade has a buyer. Separate scales do not compare dollar magnitudes across metrics.

Two separate linear bar scales use reported indicator values: spot volume 5.6 and 6.4 USD billions, CVD +6.6 and -91.8 USD millions. CVD bars share a zero baseline. Percentage change is Glassnode's reported 13.3%, not recomputed from rounded bar values.

Weekly report comparison, not Monday US-session data. Spot volume is an indicator, not a stated calendar-week total. Negative CVD concerns aggressive order flow within measured activity; every trade has a buyer. Separate scales do not compare dollar magnitudes across metrics.

Farside Investors' completed Nov. 2-6 sessions show four aggregate outflow days and one inflow day, totaling $1,282 million in net outflows. Another $201 million left the funds on Nov. 9.

Glassnode put futures open interest at $37 billion, down 1.1% from $37.4 billion seven days earlier, and described the unwind as partial. Funding payments remained elevated while short-term holders were near break-even.

A price recovery would carry more weight if improving spot CVD showed buyers taking the initiative, while positive ETF flows persisted across completed sessions.

In its Nov. 4 analysis, Glassnode placed the short-term holder cost basis at $75,700 and the True Market Mean at $77,700. The first estimates what newer holders paid, while the second estimates the cost basis of active investors.

Below, Glassnode identified a broader $73,000-$76,000 retest band and said a sustained break below $73,000 would point to longer consolidation.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics is scheduled to release October CPI today. A favorable inflation reading could ease rate expectations and support risk appetite. As the Federal Reserve explains, expected policy rates affect broader interest rates and financial conditions.

Reuters reported Bitcoin's late-Sunday fall to $73,708 amid investor worries that a Democratic-controlled Congress would toughen crypto regulation. Those concerns offer a separate reason for caution alongside the trading data.

Chelsea have revived their interest in Arsenal midfielder Martin Zubimendi ahead of the January transfer window but face competition from Real Madrid and Atletico Madrid.

Zubimendi enjoyed a bright start to his Arsenal career following a £60m move from Real Sociedad in 2025 but became a less influential figure in Mikel Arteta’s side towards the end of last season.

The 27-year-old appeared fatigued in the second-half of the campaign and lost his place in the team, with a then 19-year-old Myles Lewis-Skelly starting the Champions League final ahead of him.

The American Express Platinum Card® is one of the most popular premium cards on the market, and it currently has an incredible welcome offer that’s as high as 175,000 bonus points, making it the ideal time to apply. In this post, I’d like to go over all the details of the current Amex Platinum bonus, including who is eligible, and why you might want to apply.

While the Amex Platinum has a steep annual fee, it also has compelling perks that will make it worthwhile for many, ranging from lounge access to credits. If you’re eligible for the current bonus, then picking up the card and giving it a try is a no brainer, in my opinion.

Link: Learn more about the American Express Platinum Card®

The Amex Platinum Card welcome offer is potentially very lucrative, with a twist. The Amex Platinum bonus is marketed as being as high as 175,000 Membership Rewards points after spending $12,000 within the first six months. That’s an unconventional approach for a welcome offer, though I appreciate that Amex has the “apply with confidence” feature, so there won’t be a hard pull unless you accept the offer.

NYSE Selects Avalanche for Tokenized Stock Settlement: Can AVAX Reclaim $9.00?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Home » Altcoins » NYSE Selects Avalanche for Tokenized Stock Settlement: Can AVAX Reclaim $9.00?

Wall Street’s tokenization push gives Avalanche an institutional boost, but AVAX technicals demand caution at important level.

In a major endorsement of enterprise blockchain infrastructure, the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) has chosen Avalanche (AVAX) technology as the core ledger for clearing and settling trades on its planned tokenized stock platform.

According to an Ava Labs statement, Avalanche will provide the core ledger technology for the clearing firm of the NYSE-affiliated digital trading platform.

Subject to regulatory approval, the platform is designed to enable 24/7 trading, clearing and onchain settlement of tokenized securities.

It will support equities issued across multiple blockchains, while Avalanche will provide the underlying settlement infrastructure, offering predictable performance and costs, controlled network access and regulatory flexibility.

The announcement comes amid intensifying competition in financial asset tokenization. The NYSE has expanded its transfer agent network to six firms, including Broadridge, Computershare and Superstate.

Nasdaq is also developing tokenized securities infrastructure, while DTCC launched its tokenization service in October, using a private Besu blockchain and the Canton Network.

Meanwhile, the SEC’s new innovation exemption is opening the door to tokenized equity trading, with OKX and ICE pursuing a separate initiative.

Following the announcement, AVAX broke out of its previous consolidation range but faced resistance at $8.80–$9.00, triggering a pullback. As of November 10, AVAX is trading at approximately $8.48, up 7.2% over 24 hours.

The rally pushed Bollinger Bands wider, signaling increased volatility. AVAX is now trading below the middle band at $8.62 and the moving-average reference at $8.56, suggesting that upward momentum has cooled.

Resistance stands at $8.60–$8.65 and $9.00–$9.28, while key support can be located around $8.00, followed by $7.70 and $7.30.

Over the next few days, AVAX’s direction will largely depend on whether buyers can reclaim $8.65.A recovery above $8.65 could put AVAX back on course to test $9.00. A break above $9.28, supported by stronger trading volume, would signal renewed bullish momentum.

Failure to regain near-term resistance could leave the token consolidating, while a break below $7.95 would expose lower support levels at $7.70 or $7.30.

The NYSE announcement strengthens Avalanche’s position in institutional tokenization, but its implications for AVAX remain less direct. The platform is expected to run on a dedicated blockchain built using Avalanche technology rather than the public Avalanche network.

The extent of any resulting demand for AVAX will depend on how the platform is implemented and whether it creates mechanisms for value to accrue to the token.

A sustained recovery could put $9.50–$10.00 within reach, with further resistance around $10.50–$11.50. However, failure to overcome overhead resistance could leave AVAX in a broader consolidation range.

The NYSE announcement is a significant development for Avalanche’s institutional prospects, but it has yet to translate into a confirmed bullish reversal for AVAX. After the initial AVAX price rally, traders are now assessing whether buying demand can sustain the move beyond the immediate resistance level.

Dogecoin (DOGE) and Shiba Inu (SHIB) Holdings Cross $1.1 Billion on Major South Korean Exchange

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Upbit remains one of the largest holders of Dogecoin and Shiba Inu on the market, being the key place where pressure on the prices of those two assets arises. According to Arkham, the South Korean exchange holds $816.81 million worth of DOGE and $284.75 million worth of SHIB, which is around $1.1 billion combined. These are its largest holdings after BTC, ETH and SOL, out of approximately $22.06 billion in total.

In coins, that's 11.766 billion DOGE and a whopping 64.134 trillion SHIB. Upbit's Dogecoin balance has been growing for years, and the current reading is essentially the highest on Arkham's chart.

Shiba Inu's numbers are even more interesting. Total SHIB exchange reserves currently sit at around 86 trillion tokens, so Upbit most likely holds about three quarters of all SHIB available on exchanges, if both data sources track the same wallets.

Shiba Inu has been struggling on the market for ages; however, there is finally an exchange outflow of approximately 144 billion SHIB in the last 24 hours. It's a drop in the ocean though: exchange reserves went down by only 0.17%, and around 183 billion SHIB still flowed into exchanges over the same period.

Prices for both assets aren't doing well, showing a similar continuous bearish dynamic, with Dogecoin slowly tumbling below $0.07. Dogecoin is currently moving around $0.07, slowly losing the threshold, while continuously moving below all major moving averages. It has already lost around 35% from its September peak. Unfortunately, the price is struggling around the bottom and there's a good chance the current period's bottom will be violated. The current price level is the lowest point on the chart in the last year.

SHIB is in the same position, trading at $0.00000443 after breaking below the ascending trendline that supported it since July. The closest support is the July low around $0.0000041, with the moving averages above it aiming downwards.

RSI for both assets is moving around 35, so the market isn't oversold yet. With this much DOGE and SHIB sitting on one exchange, selling from Upbit users could add even more pressure on the price, unfortunately.

Coinbase, Kalshi join lawsuit backing CFTC over US stock perp futures

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Coinbase, Kalshi and four other companies backed the CFTC in a lawsuit over whether US exchanges can list perpetual futures tied to individual stocks.

Coinbase, Kalshi and four other companies asked a federal court Monday to accept a brief backing the United States Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) in a lawsuit over perpetual futures tied to individual US stocks.

According to a proposed amicus brief shared Monday by Coinbase legal representative Molly Abraham, the group also includes Kraken parent Payward, trading companies DRW Holdings and Jump Trading, and crypto investment company Paradigm Operations. The companies argue that stock perpetual futures fall under existing US futures laws.

The filing comes days after a federal judge found that the CFTC likely “exceeded its statutory authority” in allowing single-stock perpetuals to trade as security futures.

The case could determine whether US exchanges can offer retail traders contracts that track individual stocks without expiring.

On Nov. 3, the CFTC announced that single-stock perpetual futures, or stock perps, could begin trading on US-regulated exchanges for the first time.

The Capital Markets Alliance Association (CMAA), a newly formed trade and advocacy group representing traditional financial market participants and whose members include Cboe and Citadel Securities, sued the CFTC last week, challenging the CFTC’s authority to allow the contracts to trade.

Stock perps allow traders to speculate on the price movements of individual stocks without owning the shares. Unlike traditional futures, they have no fixed expiration date.

The CMAA argues that because the contracts lack a fixed expiration date, they should be regulated as security-based swaps, a type of derivative linked to stocks or other securities that is primarily overseen by the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) and faces tighter restrictions on retail trading.

The six companies backing the CFTC disagree, arguing that a fixed expiration date is not required for a contract to qualify as a security future, which falls under joint CFTC and SEC oversight.

Related: Coinbase’s US national trust bank charter takes effect

Following the CMAA’s complaint, US District Judge Valerie Caproni said the plaintiff was likely to succeed in its claim that the CFTC exceeded its statutory authority by allowing stock perps to trade as security futures.

Caproni found that the contracts appeared to qualify as swaps rather than security futures, casting doubt on the agency’s legal interpretation.

However, she denied CMAA’s request for a temporary restraining order, finding that the group had failed to demonstrate imminent, irreparable harm. The decision left the CFTC’s trading approvals in place while the lawsuit proceeds.

CMAA is scheduled to file its reply to the CFTC’s opposition to a preliminary injunction on Tuesday.

Anthropic sets IPO price range of $70 to $78, looks to start trading Nov. 19, sources say

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Anthropic said it will offer 825 million shares in its upcoming IPO at a price range between $70 to $78 per share, according to an updated filing on Tuesday, bringing the five-year-old artificial intelligence lab a step closer to a potentially historic Nasdaq debut.

The company is planning to price on Nov. 18 and trade on Nov. 19, according to people familiar with the details who were granted anonymity because they were not authorized to speak publicly. Anthropic is targeting retail allocation of about 20%, the people said, a much higher percentage than the typical IPO, which is 5% to 10% for retail investors.

At the high end of the range, Anthropic would net up to $64.35 billion in proceeds through the IPO. The company could end up being worth up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis, up from the $965 billion valuation it announced as part of a funding round in May.

Anthropic said it has entered into an agreement with Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company to purchase $26 billion of the company's Class N common stock in a private placement at a price per share equal to the IPO price. The shares sold in the private placement will not be registered in the offering and will not be subject to lock-up agreements with the underwriters, the company said.

As Anthropic executives get set to pitch the offering to investors, they have to contend with a tumultuous political environment, following last week's midterm elections that included a Democratic sweep in Congress and the passage of numerous statewide ballot measures that could slow the AI buildout. They're also facing a public market that's grown increasingly skeptical of the AI trade on concern that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for companies that need it for costly infrastructure purchases.

CNBC reported last week that some Anthropic investors were bracing for a lower valuation than had previously been expected. While Anthropic had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation until recently, shareholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

Should Anthropic reach the Nasdaq this month as planned, under ticker symbol "ANT," the debut would come roughly five months after SpaceX went public in a record offering that raised $85.7 billion. Elon Musk's reusable rocket and AI company has a market cap of just under $2 trillion, around its valuation at the time of the IPO.

Institutional demand for Anthropic is even stronger than it was for SpaceX at this point in the process, the people said. The company's book is already covered at the $70 to $78 per share range on an allocable basis, they said.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the AI lab in 2021 after he and a small group of researchers defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and the company primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

On Monday, Anthropic introduced two new AI models, Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2, the first time the company has released so-called "frontier" models since Amodei called to slow down the pace of AI development in September. Anthropic said the models went through rigorous safety and alignment testing, and that the company developed new evaluations and post-launch monitoring tools ahead of the release.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic said in its amended filing on Tuesday that its run-rate revenue was roughly $81 billion at the end of October, up $10 billion from the previous month.

"Our aim is to help humankind chart a safe course through this technological evolution, open new frontiers of discovery, and bring forth an age of miracles," Anthropic's co-founders wrote in the prospectus. "Everyone who owns our stock, whether an institution or an individual, becomes part of that effort. We hope you will share our ambition to see it though."

Brit Arrested After Flight Attendant Finds Hidden Camera In Airplane Bathroom, Now Faces Voyeurism And Cover-Up Charges

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A flight attendant discovered a hidden camera while using the lavatory on a Scoot flight from Japan to Singapore. A British passenger now faces charges of voyeurism and obstructing justice, with police alleging that he remotely activated the camera and later tried to erase evidence after realizing it had been found.

Hugh Lutley, a 52-year-old British national, was charged on November 5, 2026, following an incident aboard Scoot flight TR821 from Osaka (KIX) to Singapore (SIN) on October 7.

He faces one charge each of voyeurism and obstruction of justice. It appears Lutley intends to plead guilty, was offered bail of S$10,000, and has a pretrial conference scheduled for November 17 (which of course is not a conviction or a sentence).

The Singapore Police Force’s account describes an alleged attempt to record people inside an aircraft toilet. Police say the passenger concealed a camera in the lavatory, left, and activated its recording function through an application on his phone.

A cabin crew member subsequently used the lavatory and discovered the device. She removed it and immediately alerted a colleague. The passenger allegedly returned to retrieve the camera, found it missing, and deleted the application and related photographs from his phone in an attempt to avoid detection.

Police detained him when the flight arrived at Singapore Changi Airport and seized his phone and the camera.

Those are statutory maximums, but the alleged attempt to destroy evidence exposes him to a charge carrying a substantially longer maximum prison term than the underlying voyeurism charge.

There is something particularly vile about placing a camera in an aircraft lavatory. Passengers and crew have little choice about where to go, and closing and locking the door should allow them to expect privacy.

I am thankful the flight attendant found the device and reported it. Discovering something like that while using the restroom must be deeply unsettling.

The published accounts do not establish how many people, if any, were recorded before the camera was discovered…the alleged installation and remote operation are disturbing enough.

Is this world so twisted that passengers must become amateur surveillance experts every time they use an airplane bathroom? If you notice an unfamiliar device or something suspicious, alert the crew promptly…yeah, I guess you should look around before you drop your drawers.

A British passenger faces criminal charges after a flight attendant found a hidden camera in a Scoot lavatory. Police allege he remotely operated it and then deleted material from his phone after discovering the camera had been removed.

The case remains before the court. For now, credit to the crew member who discovered the device and acted. An tight aircraft lavatory may offer very little comfort, but privacy ought to be a given.
